QUESTIONS PRESENTED 


The City of Culver City assessed Richard Ehrlich two 
fees in exchange for a specific plan amendment that was a 
necessary prerequisite for building a 30-unit residential con- 
dominium complex on the former site of a lawfully demol- 
ished private health and tennis club. The first fee levied was 
for $280,000, the cost of four public tennis courts. The second 
fee of $33,200 was for public art. Ultimately, after Ehrlich 
appealed and this Court granted certiorari and remanded the 
case, the California Supreme Court upheld the concept, but 
not the amount, of the recreation fee and upheld the arts fee. 


1. Is it permissible under the Takings Clause of the 
United States Constitution to find that the public obtains a 
right in private property when the government imposes a 
restriction on the use of that property? Does it follow that if 
the restriction is removed then the property owner can be 
required to pay a fee in order to compensate the public for the 
public’s “loss” of its right created by the prior governmental 
land use restriction? 


2. May a government impose a fee upon a property 
owner as a condition to obtaining a specific plan amendment 
and escape heightened judicial scrutiny under the Takings 
Clause of the United States Constitution because the fee was 
authorized by legislation that is applicable to all similarly 
situated property owners? 


3. Is there an “aesthetic regulation” exception to the 
Takings Clause of the United States Constitution wherein a 
requirement that a property owner pay for or build a public art 
project, subject to city approval, as a condition 
of obtaining a specific plan amendment is not subject to 


a i a | 


et a 


QUESTIONS PRESENTED - Continued 


heightened judicial scrutiny as required by Nollan v. Califor- 
nia Coastal Commission, 483 U.S. 825 (1987) and Dolan v. 
City of Tigard, 512 U.S. __, 114 S.Ct. 2309 (1994)? 


4. Does requiring a property owner to build and main- 
tain public art upon his property, or pay a ransom in the form 
of an in-lieu fee, comprise a physical invasion violative of the 
Takings Clause of the United States Constitution?

PETITION FOR WRIT OF CERTIORARI 


Richard K. Ehrlich, as Trustee of the 1985 Ehrlich Fam- 
ily Trust and as Trustee of the July 28, 1965 Trust (Ehrlich), 
petitions that a writ of certiorari issue to review the published 
opinion of the California Supreme Court issued on March 5, 
1996, with a rehearing denied on April 11, 1996.

case are reproduced in the Appendix to this petition as fol- 
lows:

JURISDICTION 


This Court has jurisdiction under 28 U.S.C. § 1257. 

The opinion of the California Supreme Court was issued 
on March 5, 1996. See App. A. After petitioner timely filed a 
petition for rehearing, the California Supreme Court denied 
that petition on April 11, 1996. See App. B. Pursuant to 
Supreme Court Rule 13, the time for filing this petition for 
writ of certiorari would run for 90 days from April 11, 1996, 
or until July 10, 1996. However, on June 6, 1996, this Court 
granted Ehrlich’s motion for an extension of time to file this 
petition to August 9, 1996.! 


STATUTES INVOLVED 


The Fifth Amendment to the United States Constitution 
states in relevant part: 

Nor [shall any person] be deprived of life, liberty, 

or property, without due process of law; nor shall 

private property be taken for public use, without 

just compensation. 

The Fourteenth Amendment, Section One, of the United 
States Constitution states in relevant part: 

[Njor shall any state deprive any person of life, 

liberty, or property, without due process of law. 


' The California Supreme Court remanded to the city the question of 
how much the public recreation “tennis court” fee should be. The issue on 
remand is not relevant to this petition because petitioner does not question 
here the amount of any tennis court fee, but whether any such fee may be 
imposed in the first place. Certiorari, therefore, is appropriate at this stage 
of the proceedings. 


3 


The entire text of Culver City Ordinance No. 88-109 is 
set forth as Appendix N. 


The entire text of Culver City Ordinance No. 89-026 is 
set forth as Appendix M. 


STATEMENT OF THE CASE 


If nothing else, the California courts are stubborn. After 
being told by this Court in Ehrlich v. City of Culver City, 512 
U.S. __, 114 S. Ct. 2731 (1994), to reconsider its earlier 
decision in this case in light of the then recent opinion in 
Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. __, 114 S. Ct. 2309 (1994), 
the California Court of Appeal proceeded to reissue a new 
opinion that was identical to the one vacated, with the excep- 
tion of the addition of several paragraphs and sentences that 
described the Dolan case. The California Supreme Court 
granted review. In its opinion, the California Supreme Court 
found that the City could legitimately levy a fee to compen- 
sate the City for the alleged loss of opportunity necessarily 
foreclosed by the approval of Ehrlich’s project, but held the 
$280,000 fee in this case amounted to a taking insofar as the 
record was devoid of facts satisfying the rough propor- 
tionality standard of Dolan. 


With respect to the $280,000 “tennis court” or recreation 
fee, the California Supreme Court held that such a fee could 
be imposed to compensate the public for the impact caused by 
the loss of the then current land use restriction of “public 
recreation.” However, the court further held that the city had 
not properly justified the exact amount of $280,000 under a 
“rough proportionality” standard. The California Supreme 
Court upheld the arts fee, saying that it was equivalent to an 
aesthetic regulation. The court also found that legislatively 
imposed development fees are generally not subject to a 
heightened scrutiny analysis under the Takings Clause of the 
United States Constitution. 


This case arises because the City of Culver City believes 
that it can use the occasion of granting a specific plan amend- 
ment as a means of satisfying its need for public amenities. 
Here the specific plan amendment allowed the construction of 


4 


condominiums on a vacant parcel of land that once had been 
the site of an abandoned and demolished private tennis and 
health club. The condominiums would be in conformity with 
the then existing general plan and zoning. The City argues 
that the granting of the specific plan amendment entitles it to 
$280,000 in order to build four new public tennis courts and 
either $33,200 for public art or $33,200 worth of acceptable 
public art. The California Supreme Court agreed inasmuch as 
it found that some recreational fee would be allowable and 
that the art fee was lawful. 


A. Property History. 


In September 1973, Ehrlich as president and sole owner 
of Downtown Development Corporation (“DDC”) executed an 
agreement to purchase the subject site. Subsequent thereto, in 
early 1974, Ehrlich prepared a site plan and architectural 
drawings for a private tennis club to be erected on the site. 
Ehrlich submitted the plans to the City for approval. Appen- 
dix 1-5. Ehrlich appeared at numerous planning commission 
and City Council meetings to answer questions and concerns 
of neighboring homeowners who vehemently opposed the use 
of the site as a recreational facility of any kind. Appendix I-S. 


The specific plan in question was adopted in August 1974 
by the City Council in lieu of the usual and customary 
procedure of site plan review. Appendix I-6. The City reports 
acknowledge that the City utilized the specific plan approach 
to “mitigate any adverse effects which might otherwise 
occur” due to the construction of Ehrlich’s recreation facility 
on the site and to insure that no other C-3 uses, including 
recreational facilities other than Ehrlich’s recreational 
facility, would be constructed and/or operated on the site. 
Appendix C-40, 41. “There is nothing in the record which 
indicates that the City imposed the specific plan limiting the 
use of the property to a private tennis club in order to 
preserve the land for future use as a tennis club or another 
form of recreational facility”. Court of Appeal dissent by 
Armstrong, J., Appendix C-40, 41, n. 1. In other words, 
Ehrlich neither agreed to, nor gave any servitude or other sort 


<a ll rh 


5 


of perpetual easement to the public for commercial recre- 
ational use of the land. 


The purpose of the specific plan was to mandate the 
conditions upon which Ehrlich or anyone else could operate 
a private tennis club business, if at all, on his property. By 
its own terms the specific plan restricted and limited the 
construction of the tennis club as well as its daily operation. 
Appendix I-3. In addition, the specific plan mandated that the 
facility be a private facility available only to a limited 
number of members. By incorporating the site plan of 
Ehrlich’s recreational facility into the specific plan, the speci- 
fic plan, in effect, imposed restrictions on the exact nature, 
including size, placement, type and location, of each and 
every improvement down to the last tree and light. Changes 
to Ehrlich’s facility were expressly prohibited. Any alteration 
of the improvements and operational procedures without 
amendment or removal of the specific plan would be unlawful 
and would result in closing of the facility as well as other 
civil and/or criminal penalties. Appendix 1-4. 


The purchase of the subject property was finalized in 
January 1975, with title vested in Ehrlich rather than DDC. 
The specific plan was amended in February 1975, permitting 
Ehrlich to build and operate the club. Appendix I-S. 


Ehrlich developed and operated the site himself, or 
through others, as a private tennis club continuously through 
August, 1988. Appendix A-4, G-4. By 1981, a number of 
different operators of the private tennis club had gone out of 
business because operation of a facility under the restric- 
tions imposed by the specific plan was not an economically 
viable use of the property. Appendix A-5, G-4. 


By 1981, Ehrlich applied to the City for a specific plan 
amendment in order to allow the development of the site as a 
commercial office building, consistent with the then existing 

C-3 zoning on the property. Appendix A-4, E-3, G-4. The 
request was denied. Appendix A-4, E-3, G-4. 


B. The Demolition. 


On or about March 23, 1989, Ehrlich with the City’s 
approval obtained a demolition permit and demolished the 
recreational facilities on the site. Ehrlich donated all of the 
still useful equipment to the City, including the tennis court 
lights, nets, and lockers. Appendix A-6. 


C. The Project Application. 


On or about September 30, 1988, Ehrlich applied to the 
City for a specific plan amendment to allow development of 
the site into a 30-unit residential condominium townhouse 
development (the “Project”). Appendix A-5, E-4, G-3. The 
Project was consistent with the existing general plan and 
C-3 zoning. Appendix E-4, G-3. Consequently, approval of 
the Project did not require the City’s approval to change or 
alter the existing zoning on the site. Appendix G-3. 


D. The City’s Interest in the Property. 


Shortly after Ehrlich’s submission of the application for 
the Project in September of 1988, the City expressed an 
interest in acquiring the site for use as a publicly owned and 
operated recreational facility. Appendix A-5, G-5. The City 
commissioned a feasibility study. The study concluded that 
Ehrlich’s existing facility could not operate at a break-even or 
profitable basis in accordance with the restrictions imposed 
by the specific plan. The report stated that a viable recre- 
ational facility would require changes to the building struc- 
ture and layout of the facility which were not permitted by the 
specific plan. The suggested changes included the removal of 
various tennis courts, destruction of the existing building, and 
the addition of at least 50 parking spaces. These changes 
would have required the acquisition through condemnation or 
purchase of land from neighboring properties, at a substantial 
expense. Appendix A-5, G-9. The City did not pursue the idea 
further. With the exception of the finding that the recreational 
facility mandated by the specific plan was not a viable use of 
the property, Ehrlich does not endorse the findings of this 


7 


study and thus did not attempt to purchase land from neigh- 
boring property owners or seek another specific plan amend- 
ment which would have allowed him to further risk his 
investment by building another club. Instead, as noted, he 
sought and obtained a demolition permit. 


E. The $280,000 Exaction. 


On April 10, 1989, the City Council disapproved 
Ehrlich’s application (the “Disapproval”). This action was 
taken despite the fact that the City staff and Planning Com- 
mission had unanimously recommended approval of the Pro- 
ject, that the Project had widespread community support, and 
that the City had already given Petitioner permission to 
demolish the existing facility. Appendix A-6, E-4, G-4. 


After the Disapproval, Ehrlich had numerous communi- 
cations with the City. Appendix E-4, G-5. Ehrlich was 
informed that no project would be approved by the City 
unless Ehrlich agreed to build new public recreational facili- 
ties for the City. Appendix A-6, E-4, G-5. 


As a direct result of Ehrlich’s discussions with the City, 
in which the issue of whether Ehrlich should himself build 
four tennis courts was raised, the City Council reconsidered 
the Disapproval on July 3, 1989. The City Council then went 
into a lengthy, closed session to discuss the Project. Appendix 
A-6, E-4, G-5. After emerging from their closed session, the 
City Council voted to approve the Project including the con- 
dition that Ehrlich pay a $30,000.00 in-lieu park land fee 
pursuant to Culver City Municipal Code section 33-E.1. 
Appendix E-4, G-5. In addition, the Council imposed a 
requirement of payment of a monetary exaction of $280,000, 
the presumed cost of four tennis courts, to be spent by the 
City Council at some date in the future for public recreational 
facilities. Appendix A-6, E-4, G-5, L-5.2 


* The First Amendment implications of this provision were not 
addressed by this Court’s prior vacation and remand in this case and will 
not be addressed. 


8 
F. The Arts in Public Places Exaction. 


Culver City Ordinance No. 88-019 (the “Art in Public 
Places Exaction”) requires Ehrlich to pay a $33,220 exaction 
to the City for Art in Public Places as a condition of obtaining 
a Final Certificate of Occupancy for the Project.? Appendix 
A-7, E-4, G-6, N-1-12. Ordinance No. 88-019 requires that art 
erected in the City pursuant to the Art in Public Places 
program be approved by the City after undergoing a critical 
City review process. /d. 


G. The Litigation. 


Ehrlich provided security for the disputed public art and 
recreation fees and filed a protest pursuant to California 
Government Code §§ 66020 and 66021. Opinion of California 
Supreme Court, Appendix A-7, 8, 10. The government code 
beginning at § 66000 provides the exclusive mechanism for 
challenging development fees which fail to meet a “reason- 
able relationship” standard. Appendix A-12-15. The protest 
filed under the government code was denied by the city. 
Appendix A-7. 


On November 3, 1989, Ehrlich filed and served the Veri- 
fied First Amended and Supplemental Petition for Writ of 
Mandate and Complaint seeking to invalidate both the 
$280,000 recreation exaction and the $33,200 Art in Public 
Places Exaction. Ehrlich argued, among other things, that 
both exactions were unconstitutional under Article I, Section 
19, of the California Constitution as well as the Fifth and 
Fourteenth Amendments to the United States Constitution. 
Appendix E-5. 


1. The Superior Court. 


The Superior Court granted a writ of mandate vacating 
the $280,000 exaction stating that the exaction: (1) constitutes 
an unequal tax discriminatorily applied, and (2) that the 


3 The fee calculated as 1% of the gross value of the project. 


ooh elma all 


| 
é 


9 


exaction constitutes a taking of private property without com- 
pensation. The court found that because the City could not 
compel Ehrlich to operate a recreational facility on the site 
there was no relationship between the Project and the need for 
public tennis courts in Culver City. The court found that the 
exaction was “simply an effort to shift the cost of providing a 
public benefit to one no more responsible for the need than 
any other taxpayer.” Appendix A-8-9, E-5, H-4. The arts fee 
was upheld because it was “within the scope of the City’s 
police power.” 


2. Court of Appeal I. 


Although the California Court of Appeal initially 
affirmed the superior court decision. upon rehearing it 
reversed and upheld the $280,000 exaction despite the court's 
acknowledgment that the $280,000 bears no relationship to 
the burdens of the Project: 


“It is important to note that the mitigation fee was 

not imposed as a condition relating to the develop- 

ment project’s burden on the community for 

increased community services, such as community 
recreational facilities . . . The mitigation fee was 
imposed to compensate the City for the benefit 
conferred on the developer by the City’s approval of 

the townhome project and for the burden on the 

community resulting from the loss of recreational 

facilities.” (Emphasis added.) Ehrlich v. Culver City 

(1993) 15 Cal.App.4th 1737, 1749-1750, Appendix 

E-9-10. 

The Court of Appeal held that the Arts In Public Places 
Exaction did not constitute a taking of private property stating 
that Culver City could under its general police powers 
“require art to be placed on developments as a condition of 
approval” and that “. . . the mere giving of an option to the 
developer to pay a fee in lieu of the placement of art on the 
project does not convert the lawful permit condition into a 
fee, an exaction .. . or a taking”. Ehrlich v. Culver City, 
supra, 15 Cal.App.4th at 1758, Appendix A-28-30. 


10 


The California Supreme Court denied review. See 15 
Cal.App.4th at 1737. Appendix F-1. 


3. The United States Supreme Court. 


On June 27, 1994, this Court granted Ehrlich’s petition 
for writ of certiorari and vacated the California Court of 
Appeal’s decision. This Court’s order read: “The judgment is 
vacated and the case is remanded to the Court of Appeal of 
California, Second Appellate District, for further consider- 
ation in light of Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. __, 114 
S.Ct. 2309.” Appendix D-1. 


4. Court of Appeal II. 


On remand, a divided Court of Appeal re-issued the same 
opinion vacated by this Court with only minor revisions, 
namely the addition of a cursory discussion of Dolan. The 
court reached the identical conclusion as in the first reported 
appellate decision (Court of Appeal I), finding that Dolan’s 
requirements were satisfied because (1) there was a “nexus” 
between the elimination of zoning that had restricted all uses 
except the existence of a private tennis club and the City’s 
need to provide public facilities, (2) the $280,000 fee was 
supported by the City’s unilateral findings that the value of 
the Project was $10,000,000, the value of the demolished 
improvements was $800,000, and that the price to construct 
four tennis courts at a new site was $275,000 to $300,000. 
Appendix C-19, 21. Compare to Appendix E-16. 

The Court of Appeal declined to apply Dolan to the Art 
in Public Places exaction, stating “[t]he in lieu art fee is not 
an adjudicative decision conditioning a developer’s permit on 
an individual parcel. Accordingly, Dolan has no applicability 
(cite omitted.).” Appendix C-34, n. 6. 


5. The California Supreme Court. 


The California Supreme Court granted review and issued 
its decision on March 5, 1996. The California court found that 
Nollan and Dolan could apply to the exaction of fees in the 


context of land use permitting, at least when fees are imposed 
on only a few property owners one at a time rather than being 
uniformly or legislatively imposed upon a “broad class” of 
property owners. Appendix A-17. The court applied a Nollan 
and Dolan analysis to the $280,000 recreational fee. Appen- 
dix A-17, 34, 37. In analyzing the $280,000 tennis court fee 
the court first noted that by granting the specific plan amend- 
ment, Ehrlich’s property could no longer be utilized as “com- 
mercial recreation.” It noted that “the discontinuation of a 
private land use may have distinctly public consequences.” 
Appendix A-34. 

In response to arguments that this was a private health 
club, the court responded that “private status alone does not 
per se erase its intrinsic public value.” Appendix A-34. “In 
short,” the court concluded, “it is well accepted in both the 
case and statutory law that the discontinuance of a private 
land use can have a significant impact justifying a monetary 
exaction to alleviate it.” Appendix A-34. The court did not 
provide any supporting citation. 

The court also noted that it had “no doubt as to the city’s 
legitimate authority to impose development impact fees for 
park and recreational purposes.” Appendix A-39. With that 
Statement, the court upheld the imposition of some sort of 
tennis court fee, finding that the fee “will substantially 
advance its legitimate interest in correcting a demonstrated 
deficiency in municipal recreational resources.” Appendix 
A-39. 

Turning to the “rough proportionality” standard of Dolan, 
the court continued that the amount of the $280,000 recre- 
ation fee had not been adequately justified. The Court found 
that the loss of private tennis courts could not be equated with 
cost of building new public courts because the private courts 
could be financed by member dues. Appendix A-41-42. While 
noting that the “city may not constitutionally measure the 
magnitude of its loss, or of the recreational exaction, by the 
value of facilities it had no right to appropriate without 
payment,” Appendix A-42, the Court continued to ponder 
what sort of fee could be imposed. It suggested that Ehrlich 
could be required to pay for the “additional administrative 


12 


expenses incurred in redesignating other property within 
Culver City for recreational use.” Appendix A-42. In other 
words, “[i]t would be reasonable to require plaintiff to con- 
tribute toward defraying these anticipated rezoning costs, so 
that the city does not have to bear them itself or pass them 
along to future private developers seeking to construct recre- 
ational facilities.” Appendix A-42. 

Expanding on the theme that a fee could be used to 
attract other private developers, the court characterized the 
restriction previously imposed upon Ehrlich’s ability to make 
use of his land as a “relinquished” land use “incentive.” The 
court suggested that the city could impose a fee of sufficient 
magnitude that “would enable the city to induce private health 
club development by offering monetary incentives roughly 
proportional to the land use incentive it relinquished when it 
removed the recreational use restriction from the plaintiff's 
property.” Appendix A-42-43. 

Earlier in the decision the court noted that a legislatively 
imposed fee would not be subject to heightened scrutiny. See 
Appendix A-29. However, in discussing the Art in Public 
Places Fee, the court did not expressly indicate whether or not 
it was analyzing the fee under the heightened scrutiny 
required by Nollan and Dolan. Instead, it expressly upheld 
this fee on the basis that it was simply an aesthetic regulation 
well within the community’s police power: “[The require- 
ment... is more akin to traditional land use regulations 
imposing minimal building setbacks, parking and lighting 
conditions, landscaping requirements, and other design condi- 
tions such as color schemes, building materials and architec- 
tural amenities.” Appendix A-45-46. 

Two members of the court dissented on the recreational 
$280,000 tennis court fee issue. They argued first that it was 
doubtful that the City could have denied the specific plan 
amendment in the first place, and therefore would not have 
had the authority to impose these exactions. They suspected 
that such a denial would have been itself a taking because it 
did not appear that a private recreational club was an econom- 
ically viable use of the land. Appendix A-83-84 (Kennard, J., 
dissenting). The dissenters found, however, that whether or 


13 


not commercial recreation was a viable economic use, the fee 
was still unconstitutional because the “lost opportunity” ratio 
nale was untenable since it would “require Ehrlich to bear a 
grossly disproportionate share of what is essentially a public 
expense.” Appendix A-87-89. Requiring Ehrlich alone to bear 
the burden of providing the public with commercial recreation 
“impermissibly singled out Ehrlich’s property for special 
treatment . . . akin to prohibited spot zoning.” Appendix 
A-87. 


REASONS FOR GRANTING REVIEW 


At bottom this case presents a fundamental philosophical 
question on the nature of property ownership: Is private 
property owned and used by an individual for the benefit of 
that individual? Or is private property held in a quasi-trust by 
the community? In other words, to what extent does an owner 
have the right, subject of course to reasonable health and 
safety related permitting requirements, to make reasonable 
use of property? Can the government condition approval of a 
property owner’s chosen use upon the payment of an indul- 
gence designed to “compensate” the community for the “loss” 
of other potential uses that are foregone when the owner is 
permitted to put the property to the owner’s chosen use? The 
opinion below of the California Supreme Court adopts the 
view that a landowner cannot make reasonable use of property 
unless consent is obtained and indulgences are paid. This is 
an unprecedented vision that is in conflict with history, tradi- 
tion, and the fundamental underpinnings of the law of prop- 
erty as recognized by this and other courts. 


This case is not about preventing any real harm to the 
community as a result of the new project. The courts below 
explicitly found that the challenged fees were not designed to 
mitigate any impacts caused by the construction of Mr. 
Ehrlich’s condominiums. Appendix C-18-19, n. 3. 


This case is not about preventing a loss of any existing 
and actual use of Mr. Ehrlich’s property by the community. 
The tennis courts, swimming pool, and aerobics rooms had 


14 


already been lawfully abandoned and demolished when these 
fees were imposed. Appendix A-6, G-4. 


This case is not about the loss of any indicia of the 
“quality of life” of the community such as open space, habi- 
tat, or the like. The granting of a specific plan amendment 
simply allowed Mr. Ehrlich to place new and different struc- 
tures upon his property where other structures had previously 
stood. There is nothing in the record that purports to justify 
the challenged fees on the basis of the loss of any natural 
features. 


Finally, this case is not even about a community’s 
attempt to establish a particular quality of life for the new 
residents of the condominiums. As interpreted by the Califor- 
nia Supreme Court, the recreational exaction was not 
designed to ameliorate any impact from the condominiums 
but is intended to compensate the public for modifying the 
restrictions previously imposed on the property. To the extent 
that the city found that the new residents would need a certain 
amount of public parks and open space, Mr. Ehrlich paid, 
without objection or protest, a $30,000 in lieu parkland fee 
for those specific purposes. Appendix A-7. Similarly, the 
record is devoid of any support that the Art in Public Places 
fee is necessary to ameliorate impacts from Ehrlich’s condo- 
minium project. 

What this case is about is an attempt to use the land use 
permitting power to force Mr. Ehrlich to pay a recreational 
fee in order to provide the community as a whole with 
recreational facilities. But the public never itself had the right 
to recreate on Ehrlich’s land without Ehrlich’s consent and 
willingness to invest in a commercial recreational enterprise. 
This case is also about whether the government can, under the 
guise of aesthetic regulation, force a landowner to reserve a 
portion of that owner’s land and construct and place art upon 
it, or pay for substitute art. 


15 
I 


IN UPHOLDING THE RECREATION FEE THE COURT 
BELOW IGNORES THE PRECEDENT OF THIS COURT 
AND IS IN CONFLICT WITH THE HOLDINGS OF 
OTHER COURTS 


A. The Imposition of a Regulatory Restriction on an 
Owner’s Right to Make Reasonable Use of Property 
Does Not Grant a “Benefit” to the Owner or Create a 
Public Right in the Property. To Hold Otherwise Con- 
flicts With Established Principles of Property Owner- 
Ship and Regulation. 


In analyzing the public recreation fee, the court below 
transformed fundamental principles of property ownership 
and the premise of government regulation of property. The 
California Supreme Court found that when government 
changes a land use restriction imposed on property that 
change can allegedly create an impact on the public such that 
the government can demand mitigation from the landowner. 
The court below found that the mitigation is tied to the 
“impact of the land use change the city granted plaintiff.” 
Appendix A-42. But the imposition of a land use restriction 
neither creates a “benefit” in the property owner nor a “right” 
in the public to that restriction. A change in that restriction, 
therefore, does not grant or take away any “benefit” to the 
owner or affect any “rights” owned by the public. Thus, the 
specific plan amendment sought here cannot create the sort of 
public harm that can justify the exactions imposed by Culver 
City. 


After determining that the exaction of a fee can implicate 
the takings clause, the California Court begins its analysis of 
the $280,000 recreation fee issue by noting that the collection 
of a fee, here for recreational use, “will substantially advance 
its legitimate interest in correcting a demonstrated deficiency 
in municipal recreational resources.” Appendix A-40. But an 
existing deficiency is not relevant to the question of whether 


16 


government can make a landowner pay for correcting a defi- 
ciency when it changes the land use restrictions previously 
imposed on the owner's property. 


With this passage, the California Court appears to be 
referring to the Takings Clause analysis that this Court 
spelled out in Agins v. City of Tiburon: 


“The application of a general zoning law to particu- 
lar property effects a taking if the ordinance does 
not substantially advance legitimate state inter- 
ests .. . or denies an owner economically viable use 
of his land [citation].” 


Agins v. City of Tiburon, 447 U.S. 255, 260 (1980) (emphasis 
added). Indeed, it was on the basis that the exactions in 
Nollan v. California Coastal Commission, 483 U.S. 825 
(1987) and Dolan v. City of Tigard, 512 U.S. __, 114 S. Ct. 
2309 (1994) failed to advance a legitimate governmental 
interest that this Court struck down the exactions in those two 
cases. But no one ever argued that some public purpose could 
not be advanced by the coastal beachfront property dedication 
in Nollan or the public greenway and bicycle path in Dolan. 
The problem was that there\ was no legitimate public purpose 
served because the exactions had not been shown to amelio- 
rate any impact caused by the development in Nollan or to 
ameliorate with enough precision the impacts alleged to have 
been caused by the development in Dolan. Likewise, no 
alleged impact caused by Ehrlich’s specific plan amendment 
can justify the exactions imposed by the City. 


The fact that Ehrlich’s property was designated as “com- 
mercial recreation” did create the “potentiality” that some- 
body might someday place some sort of new commercial 
recreation facility on the property. But even if that poter- 
tiality were significant, that would not be enough to find any 
legitimate state interest in requiring Ehrlich to pay for public 


* The fact that Ehrlich lost money on such an enterprise for over a 
dozen years makes such a potentiality vanishingly small — at least on 
Ehrlich’s parcel without the purchase of neighboring property and 
obtaining a new specific plan amendment. 


a 


cre” 


17 


recreation because Ehrlich was not responsible for the “dem- 
onstrated deficiency in municipal recreational resources.” Just 
because the government previously imposed a “commercial 
recreation” restriction on the use of his property does not 
mean that the government also imposed upon Ehrlich the duty 
to actually provide such “commercial recreation” or even the 
potential of such recreation. The imposition of a land use 
restriction is not the same thing as the imposition of a duty to 
provide the use allowed by the restriction. 


It is important to reiterate that this fee was not imposed 
because of any impact of the development of the condomin- 
iums themselves. Appendix C-18-19, n.3. The fee was 
allegedly designed to mitigate for the loss of the preexisting 
land use restriction - even though the facilities had already 
been lawfully removed. Appendix C-18. Under the reasoning 
of the court below, every time the zoning or land use restric- 
tion of property changes the public loses something — whether 
that be the lost recreational opportunities of this case, or lost 
employment opportunities if commercial property is rezoned 
to recreational, the lost food opportunities if agricultural land 
is rezoned as commercial recreational, or the lost housing 
opportunities if residential land is rezoned into anything else. 
Furthermore, it would make no difference who initiated the 
change in the governmentally imposed land use restriction, 
whether it be the owner, the planning staff, or neighbors. 
Every time the use of land is changed the public theoretically 
loses some potentiality that the landowner could be required 
to mitigate. 


The fact that Culver City allegedly has a pre-existing 
shortage of commercial recreational facilities may be unfortu- 
nate, but it is not Ehrlich’s fault or sole responsibility. There 
is nothing in the record of this case that suggests that Ehrlich 
had undertaken any sort of duty to provide public recreation. 
At the time the city council imposed the $280,000 recre- 
ational fee, the tennis courts on his property were long gone 
with the City’s approval. There is absolutely no precedent in 
the law of California or elsewhere for the notion that once 
property is impressed with a particular land use restriction the 
community owns a perpetual easement or servitude in the 


18 


continuation of that use — whether or not the property is 
actually being put to that particular use. 

As noted by Professor Kmiec, it would be an “extraordin- 
ary notion” “that once a private landowner has undertaken a 
permitted common law use, like [construction of] a private 
swimming pool or tennis court, he either must continue that 
use Or must pay to stop.” Kmiec, At Last, the Supreme Court 
Solves the Takings Puzzle, 19 Harv. J.L. & Pub. Policy 147, 
156, n. 43 (1995). 


B. The Imposition of a Duty on a Land Owner to Provide 
Previously Zoned Land Uses Would Entail a Redefini- 
tion of Property Rights in California - a Result Pro- 
scribed by This Court. 


It is beyond debate that under the law of California a 
property owner such as Ehrlich has no right to the existing 
zoning on his undeveloped property. See, e.g., Avco Commu- 
nity Developers, Inc. v. South Coast Regional Commission, 
132 Cal.Rptr. 386, 393, 17 Cal.3d 785, 796 (Cal. 1976) (“[iJt 
is beyond question that a landowner has no vested right in 
existing or anticipated zoning.”) If the community legit- 
imately determines that it is in the public interest to rezone 
property the owner of undeveloped property has no right to 
prevent the rezoning — except in those rare circumstances 
where a downzoning may be so severe as to deny economi- 
cally viable use of the property.> 

Under the decision of the California Supreme Court, the 
public has been given greater expectations in the existing 
zoning than an owner. The owner has no right to build in a 
way that is compatible with current zoning, but the public has 
a right to an exaction from the property owner if the commu- 
nity determines that a new zoning or land use restriction is 
appropriate. In effect, the public would own a servitude over 
all property with any governmentally imposed land use 
restriction. To create this servitude all the government has to 


5 Spot zoning or zoning for arbitrary or capricious reasons would not, 
of course, be legitimate.

do is zone a parcel of property. Under existing law, the 
property owner would generally receive no compensation 
when the servitude is imposed, Avco, supra. Now, however, 
according to the opinion below, the owner will have to pay 
when it is removed or changed. 

To find that such a servitude exists would be nothing less 
than a dramatic rewriting of real property law in California.® 
Such a redefinition of property would be at odds with this 
Court’s proscriptions in Lucas v. So. Carolina Coastal Coun- 
cil, 50S U.S. ___, 112 S. Ct. 2886, 2892 (1992) (in context of 
physical invasions “the government's power to redefine the 
range of interests included in the ownership of property was 
necessarily constrained by constitutional limits”); Hughes v. 
State of Washington, 389 U.S. 290, 296-97 (1967) (Stewart, 
J., concurring) (“a State cannot be permitted to defeat the 
constitutional prohibition against taking property without due 
process of law by the simple device of asserting retroactively 
that the property it has taken never existed at all”); See also 
Chicago, Burlington & Quincy Railroad Co. v. City of Chi- 
cago, 166 U.S. 226, 235 (1897) (noting that if the final 
judgment of a state court results in a taking of property that 
would be a violation of the 14th Amendment.) 

The California Supreme Court's holding undermines fun- 
damental principles of property ownership and regulation and 
will lead to untenable results. The settled expectations held 
by property owners for generations have been changed. The 
court below has developed a new two-part rule of property: 
(1) Once property is restricted for a particular use it must 
always be restricted for that particular use — unless the gov- 
ernment changes the restriction and (2) if the government 


© The rights in property have long been considered more than simply 
the right to the present use. “An important element in a property owners’s 
bundle of rig’ ‘s is the right to economically sell the land for the best price 
available in the market, based not only on its current use but on potential 
other uses.” Brown v. United States, 73 F.3d 1100 (Fed. Cir. 1996) citing 
United States v. Causby, 328 U.S. 256, 261 (1946) (“[mJarket 
value . . . may reflect the use to which the land could readily be converted, 
as well as the existing use.”) (emphasis added). 


20 


changes the land use restriction it can charge the owner for 
the change in order to provide the pubic with the opportunity 
to enjoy any uses that were not forbidden by the prior restric- 
tion. 


This rule, in accordance with the opinion below, would 
apply to any type of use to which property had previously 
been put, or could have been put, in conformance with pre- 
vious land use restrictions. Suppose that the land use restric- 
tions on three parcels of property allowed for the 
community’s only grocery store, law office, or a saloon, 
respectively. To paraphrase the decision below, if the land use 
restrictions were changed so that only different uses were 
allowed, the owner might have to pay, “a fee that would 
enable the city to induce private [grocery store, law office, or 
saloon] development by offering monetary incentives roughly 
proportional to the land use incentive it relinquished when it 
removed the [grocery store, law office, or saloon] use restric- 
tion from [the owner’s] property.” Appendix A-43. Theo- 
retically, this could mean a fee for grocery delivery charges, a 
legal advice hotline, or lost “socialization” opportunities rep- 
resented by the loss of an adequate community liquor supply. 


Indeed, as will be shown next, the notion that certain fees 
can be charged when a use restriction on property is removed 
or changed is in conflict with the holdings of other state 
courts. 


C. There is a Conflict Among State Courts Regarding the 
Degree to Which Property Owners Can Be Forced to 
Pay Fees When Existing Uses are Discontinued. 


What if Ehrlich had built rental housing and the market 
collapsed so that he could no longer obtain an appropriate 
return on his investment? Could he be required to pay a 
bounty to the city for its lost housing opportunities if he 
requested a change in land use that would allow him to stop 
renting apartments or to build commercial recreation? Indeed, 
it is on this very question that the courts of California and 
New York are in conflict. Implicit in the decision below, and 
explicit in prior California appellate court decisions (cited in 


21 


a concurring opinion in Ehrlich, Appendix A-72 (Mosk, J. 
concurring)) is the notion that owners of rental property can 
be forced to pay a tribute in order to exit the rental business. 
See e.g. Terminal Plaza Corp. v. City and County of San 
Francisco, 177 Cal.App.3d 892 (1986) (upholding ordinance 
requiring payment of tenant “relocation assistance” and con- 
structing, rehabilitating, or paying for one-for-one replace- 
ment of converted rental housing prior to converting rental 
units to any other use). 


In two other cases, courts have upheld low income hous- 
ing fees after downplaying the impact of this Court’s recent 
takings decisions. In Commercial Builders of Northern Cali- 
fornia v. City of Sacramento, 941 F.2d 872, 874 (9th Cir. 
1991) cert. denied 118 L.Ed.2d 593 (1992) the Ninth Circuit 
suggested that the level of scrutiny did not change as a result 
of Nollan, at least where a “physical encroachment on land” 
was not involved. Similarly, in Garneau v. City of Seattle, 897 
F. Supp. 1318, 1325 (W.D. Wash. 1995) a district court upheld 
a tenant relocation fee after speculating that Nollan and 
Dolan did not change the level of scrutiny when physical 
encroachments were not involved. 


However, the notion that such relocation fees were con- 
stitutional was explicitly rejected in Seawall Associates v. The 
City of New York, 74 N.Y.2d 92, 544 N.Y. Supp. 2d 542, 542 
N.E.2d 1059 (1989) where New York’s Court of Appeals held 
that fees imposed on the owners of single-room occupancy 
hotel were unconstitutional because imposing the financial 
burden of providing low cost housing solely on the owners of 
these units failed to substantially advance a legitimate gov- 
ernmental interest and was, therefore, an unconstitutional 
taking without just compensation under the standards of Agins 
and Nollan. 


These cases are different from the present case in one 
significant respect. In Terminal Plaza the rental use was an 
existing use at the time the owners wished to change the use. 
In Seawall the rental building existed, although it was empty 
at the time. Here, of course, the use had already been lawfully 
abandoned and only vacant property remained. Because there

3 
pe ce ome 


22 


is a conflict among states in those situations where a govern- 
ment attempts to impose a servitude on a landowner attempt- 
ing to abandon an existing use, which the public was to “lose” 
the opportunity to enjoy, there is surely even more of a 
conflict with the present case, where the use had already been 
lawfully terminated.’ 


7 The California Supreme Court found that heightened scrutiny 
applies to the imposition of monetary exactions imposed on the use of 
property, but with other courts questioning the relevance of the analysis of 
Nollan and Dolan to exactions other than real property exactions, there is a 
confusion in the lower courts that affects all landowners. This Court should 
grant review to resolve the conflict. 


For example, in City of Portsmouth v. Schlesinger, 46 F.3d 133, 135 
(ist Cir. 1995) the court applied Dolan to the imposition of a fee, finding 
that “no rational nexus existed between the amount of the Developers’ 
promissory note and any burden imposed on the City due to the zoning 
change.” And in Christopher Lake Development Co. v. St. Louis County, 35 
F.3d 1269 (8th Cir. 1994) the imposition of excessive drainage mitigation 
fees was found to violate the Takings Clause; See also Northern Illinois 
Home Builders Assoc. v. County of Du Page, 649 N.E.2d 384 (Ill. 1995) 
(transportation development fee); Home Builders Association of Central 
Arizona v. City of Scottsdale, 902 P.2d 1347, 1350 (1994) (fees can be 
subject to heightened scrutiny); and Trimen Development Co. v. King 
County, 877 P.2d 187, 194 (Wash. 1994) (park fee struck down). See also 
Aspen-Tarpon Springs, Ltd. v. George Stuart, 635 So.2d 61 (Fla. App. 
1994) (Florida Mobile Home Act's relocation expenses found 
unconstitutional taking that unfairly singled out park owners thus failing to 
substantially advance legitimate governmental interest test of Agins). See 
also, Jason Biggs, Nollan and Dolan: The End of Municipal Land Use 
Extortion — A California Perspective, 36 Santa Ciara L. Rev. 515, 
(1996); but see McCarthy v. City of Leawood, 894 P.2d 836, 845 (Kansas, 
1995) (finding Dolan does not apply to imposition of transportation fees).

Il 


EXCEPTING LEGISLATIVELY IMPOSED FEES FROM 
HEIGHTENED SCRUTINY IS AN UNCONSTITU- 
TIONAL EVASION OF THE PRINCIPLES OF NOLLAN 
AND DOLAN 


A. All Monetary Exactions Imposed in Exchange for a 
Change in Land Use Are Subject to Heightened Scru- 
tiny, Whether or Not They Are Individually or Legis- 
latively Imposed. 


Thus, the Supreme Court of California got it right when 
it found that certain fees such as the tennis court fee should 
be subject to heightened scrutiny. But its analysis broke down 
when it found that the only rationale for the heightened 
scrutiny was that the “discretionary context” of the permitting 
process “presents an inherent and heightened risk that local 
government will manipulate the police power to impose con- 
ditions unrelated to legitimate land use regulatory ends.” 
Appendix A-17. This may be true, but there is no more of a 
risk to the landowner of governmental overreaching when a 
permit condition is concocted during the “give and take” of 
the permitting process then when the condition is created in 
advance by a city council looking for off-budget methods of 
financing public projects.’ Indeed, some of the aforemen- 
tioned cases relied upon in Dolan involved legislatively 
imposed fees. See e.g. Collis v. Bloomington, 246 N.W.2d 19, 
26 (Minn. 1976) (striking down a legislatively imposed 10% 
land or in lieu fee exaction); Call v. West Jordan, 202 P.2d 
217 (Utah 1979) (striking down legislatively imposed 7% 
land or in lieu exaction). 


’ The danger was described in Pennell v. C ity of San Jose, 485 U.S. 1, 
21, 108 S. Ct. 849, 863 (1988) (Scalia, J. dissenting) (“[t}he politically 
attractive feature of regulation is not that it permits wealth transfers to be 
achieved that could not be achieved otherwise; but rather that it permits 
them to be achieved ‘off budget,’ with relative invisibility and thus relative 
immunity from normal democratic processes.”’) 


24 


In fact it is arguable that there is more of a risk of the 
arbitrary and confiscatory imposition of fees in the legislative 
context when government imposes exactions on the develop- 
ment of new residential housing because the prospective resi- 
dents who will bear the costs of the exactions may not yet be 
voting citizens in the community. 


Since Dolan, several other state courts have subjected the 
imposition of legislatively imposed development fees to the 
heightened scrutiny standards of Dolan and Nollan. See e.g. 
Northern Illinois Home Builders Assoc. v. County of Du Page, 
649 N.E.2d 384 (Ill. 1995) (utilizing Dolan analysis to strike 
down legislatively imposed transportation development fee); 
Trimen Development Co. v. King County, 877 P.2d 187, 194 
(Wash. 1994) (applying Dolan takings analysis to legis- 
latively imposed park impact development fee)’; See also J.C. 
Reeves Corp. v. Clackamas County, 131 Ore. App. 220 (Ore- 
gon App. 1994) (land dedication requirement subject to Tak- 
ings Clause even if legislatively imposed). 


Other courts, however, have found that legislatively 
imposed exactions are immune to heightened scrutiny takings 
analysis. None of these courts, however, can be accused of 
analyzing in detail the impact of the dedication on the prop- 
erty owner’s ability to exercise the right to use property rather 
than relying on a formalistic “legislative” versus “adjudica- 
tive” distinction. See e.g. Home Builders Association of Cen- 
tral Arizona v. City of Scottsdale, 902 P.2d at 1351 (Ariz. 
1995) (although fees are subject to takings analysis, if legis- 
latively imposed they will survive challenge); Southeast Cass 
Water Resource District v. Burlington Northern Railroad Co., 
527 N.W.2d 884, 896 (North Dakota 1995) (requirement that 
railroad pay for water improvement related infrastructure 
upheld against takings challenge because legislatively 
imposed). 


9 For an analysis of the takings implications of legislatively imposed 
fees in Washington State, see Note and Comment, Joseph Lee, Sudden 
Impact: The Effect of Dolan v. City of Tigard on Impact Fees in 
Washington, 71 Wasn. L. Rev. 205 (1996).

In any event, the distinction of “legislative” versus 
“adjudicatory” imposition of fees will be a chimerical one at 
best. A line has been drawn by the court below. On one side, 
for exactions imposed pursuant to legislative processes, the 
heightened scrutiny test will not apply. On the other side, for 
fee exactions imposed as part of the “give and take” of the 
adjudicative process, the heightened scrutiny standard will 
apply. 

This line is meaningless in California where virtually all 
significant land use decisions are “legislative.” In this case 
the California Supreme Court found that the recreation fee 
was imposed as part of the adjudicative process and therefore 
deserved heightened scrutiny review. What the court below 
forgot was that it had previously ruled, repeatedly, that speci- 
fic plan amendments are legislative acts, not adjudicatory. See 
e.g. Yost v. Thomas, 36 Cal.3d 561, 570 (1984) (rezoning of 
land, the adoption and amendment of specific plans are all 
legislative acts); Arnel Development Co. v. City of Costa 
Mesa, 28 Cal.3d 511, 521 (1980) (amendment of zoning 
ordinance a legislative act). 


To the extent that the court below was relying on the 
distinction between legislative and adjudicative acts based 
upon the broad applicability of the former, this has no rele- 
vance to the question of whether a particular fee is at all 
related to any legitimate impacts caused by the use of prop- 
erty. Even broadly applicable land use regulations can be 
highly abusive.'° In Avco, for example, the California 
Supreme Court held that a legislative change in land use 
restrictions would apply even to a developer who had 
expended over two million dollars in reliance upon an exis- 
ting tract map and grading permit. Avco, 17 Cal.3d at 790. 
The lack of the “give and take” of the permitting process in 


'© Indeed, in Nollan the Coastal Commission applied a broadly 
applicable regulation in that it had “similarly conditioned 43 out of 60 
coastal development permits along the same tract of land” and the other 17 
were not on the beachfront or were approved prior to the regulations. 483 
U.S. at 829. 


26 


the context of a legislatively imposed exaction is no bulwark 
against abusive governmental action. 


It is critical in this regard to note that the “heightened 
scrutiny” standard flows from the fact that government is 
attempting to restrict the exercise of the right to use real 
property. It is not dependent on the nature or origin of the 
particular condition imposed on the use of property. In other 
words, what matters is that an owner is being restricted from 
exercising the right to use real property unless the owner 
provides some sort of bounty. It does not matter whether the 
particular bounty is an exaction of land or of money, or that 
the exaction is imposed individually by a city council or 
pursuant to a general ordinance. 

What is important is not that the condition is imposed in 
the context of an individualized proceeding, but that it is 
imposed at all in the context of an individual attempting to 
make lawful use of property while the government says “no — 
not unless you comply with our conditions.” Individually 
created or broadly applicable, to a landowner it boils down to 
the same thing, a person is being forced to provide an exac- 
tion to the community that is unrelated or only loosely related 
to any impact caused by the actual use of the property. This is 
the fundamental reason for heightened scrutiny, and not the 
origin of the exaction or even the nature of the precise thing 
that is being exacted. With this case, this Court can clarify 
once and for all the rationale behind its holdings in Nollan 
and Dolan and their applicability to Ehrlich’s situation. 


B. There Is No Aesthetic Regulation Exception to the 
Takings Clause. 


The California Supreme Court upheld the Art in Public 
Places fee because it was “more akin to traditional land use 
regulations imposing minimal building setbacks, parking and 
lighting conditions, landscaping requirements, and other 
design conditions such as color schemes” which are “valid 
exercises of the city’s traditional police power.” Appendix 
A-46. This is far too facile an analysis. All the conditions 
imposed in aesthetic type regulations generally inure to the 


27 


benefit of the owner’s property, whether or not the owner 
finds them desirable. This is part of the “average reciprocity 
of advantage” so fundamental to modern land use regulation. 
See, e.g., Pennsylvania Coal v. Mahon, 260 U.S. 393, 414 
(1922). But the imposition of a fee for public art, which might 
or might not be placed on or near the subject property (here it 
was) inures more to the benefit of the community than the 
property itself thus requiring more than a simple inquiry into 
whether the regulation is consistent with the police power. 

In Nollan, the beachfront dedication surely would have 
benefitted the public, and acquiring the beach would have 
been arguably a iegitimate exercise of the police power. 
Nevertheless, the beach dedication condition was found viola- 
tive of the constitution. In Dolan, the greenway and bikepath 
would similarly have relieved a perceived flood danger and 
traffic problem, and the acquisition of the facilities would 
have been within the exercise of the police power. But again, 
the exactions were struck down because the city had not 
proven that Mrs. Dolan should pay for them. In Lucas, this 
Court found that just because the regulation served an impor- 
tant public purpose within the scope of the police power it 
would not be immune from takings analysis unless the regula- 
tion served to prevent a genuine nuisance. 

Here, just because public art supposedly benefits the 
public, and because the thrusting of such art upon the public 
is arguably within the scope of the police power, does not 
immunize this scheme from a takings analysis. There is no 
“aesthetic regulation” exception to the Takings Clause. 

The relevant inquiry should have been whether the 
requirement that Ehrlich set aside 1% of the value of the 
proposed project for art forced him “alone to bear public 
burdens which, in all fairness and justice, should be borne by 
the public as a whole.” Armstrong v. United States, 364 U.S. 
40, 49 (1960). A Takings Clause inquiry here cannot be 
abandoned until two questions are answered. First, a court 
Should inquire whether the land use at issue creates any need 
for public art. Second it must be asked whether requiring a 
property owner to acquire, construct, place, and maintain 
public art, or pay an in-lieu fee, is imposing a burden on the 


28 


use of his property that is out of proportion to legitimate 
public concerns and and impacts created by the use of the 
property. Indeed, in this case, the very fact that the ordinance 
allows landowners to “buy” their way out of the public art 
requirement leads to the conclusion that the public interest is 
perhaps less critical than it is for other land use restrictions 
that lack such a buy out provision. See Seawall, 542 N.E.2d at 
1069. 


Review is necessary in this case in order to determine 
whether the imposition of exactions are immune from Takings 
Clause analysis simply because the exactions mirror aesthetic 
regulations that are within the police power. 


C. A Property Owner Who is Required to Either Pay an 
Extortionate Fee or Place Public Art on His Property 
Has Been Subject to a Physical Invasion. 


This Court has always found physical invasions of prop- 
erty to be takings, whether the invasion be by flooding, 
Pumpelly v. Green Bay & Mississippi Canal Company, 80 
U.S. 166 (1872), boaters, Kaiser Aetna v. United States, 444 
U.S. 164, 179-80 (1979), or cable boxes, Loretto v. Tele- 
prompter Manhattan CATV Corp., 458 U.S. 419 (1982). It is 
not the amount of land that counts or even the money repre- 
sented. Loretto certainly involved a very smail invasion. The 
principle is not dependent upon whether or not people are 
able to trespass upon the property. In Pumpelly and Loretto 
there was no significant trespass by people. Instead, what 
matters in these cases is that each one represents a situation 
where the owner has lost the fundamental dominion over the 
affected land that is the sine qua non of property ownership. 
As this Court found in Loretto, the injury to the owner occurs 
when the owner “must permit another to exercise complete 
dominion” over a portion of the owner’s land. Put another 
way, “[t]he Government does not have the right to declare 
itself a cotenant-in-possession with a property owner.” 
Hendler v. United States, 952 F.2d 1364 (Fed. Cir. 1991). The 
owner no longer has any choice over what can be done with 
the property and the owner can no longer choose to exclude 


eee eae ee 


ta XR ab SE = ate 


29 


trespassers or objects from his land. The government has 
made the choice already. In essence, the owner is no longer 
the owner. 


The placement of public art, or a requirement that an 
owner pay a ransom so that art can be placed somewhere else, 
is no different. Either the owner submits involuntarily to 
losing all control and dominion over a portion of his property, 
or he can buy his way out. This Court has already made it 
plain that citizens do not have to buy their rights already 
guaranteed by the Constitution. “Under the well-settled doc- 
trine of ‘unconstitutional conditions,’ the government may 
not require a person to give up a constitutional right — here 
the right to receive just compensation when property is taken 
for a public use — in exchange for a discretionary benefit 
conferred by the government where the property sought has 
little or no relationship to the benefit.” Dolan, 114 S. Ct. at 
2317. Here the analysis is the same. Culver City cannot force 
Ehrlich to give up his constitutional right to exclude public 
art from his property or to force him to pay a fee when both 
the art and the fee are unrelated to any impact caused by the 
project.!! 

This Court has been zealous in protecting individuals 
from being made to suffer physical invasions of their property 
without the payment of just compensation. Here the only way 
Ehrlich could have avoided a physical invasion is if he paid 
compensation to the city. This may be a clever attempt to 


'! Another reason why the Art in Public Places Fee should perhaps be 
subjected to heightened scrutiny under the Takings Clause is because the 
combination of the First Amendment implications of this case with the 
takings implications. See Employment Division, Dept. of Human Resources 
of Oregon v. Smith, 494 U.S. 872, 881-82 (1990) (regulations that implicate 
“the Free Exercise Clause in conjunction with other [First Amendment 
related] constitutional protections” receive higher scrutiny) and Keller v, 
State Bar of California, 496 U.S. 1, 11 (1990) (the right not to speak 
protected by the first Amendment.) Here Ehrlich has been forced to either 
pay a fee or to display art against his will and that is subject to city 
approval. 


30 


avoid the constitution, but it is not an appropriate one. This 
Court should grant certiorari. 


CONCLUSION 


Ehrlich has been to this Court before. He is being forced 
to pay a recreation or tennis court fee in exchange for a 
specific plan amendment that would change the use of his 
vacant land from commercial recreation to residential. He is 
being forced to accept public art on his property or pay the 
City of Culver City a fee so it can place public art elsewhere. 
In both cases there is no showing that Ehriich is responsible 
for either the public “need” of the tennis courts or the art. 
Culver City and the court below have transformed the law of 
property and zoning in California into the law of the conve- 
nient patsy. This Court should accept this petition for writ of 
certiorari to resolve conflicts with decisions of this Court and 
with decisions of other states and the federal courts.

RICHARD K. EHRLICH, )

(Filed Mar. 5, 1996) 


This case comes to us by a circuitous route, having 
been remanded after the United States Supreme Court 
issued a writ of certiorari to the Court of Appeal and 
vacated that court’s judgment in favor of defendant City 
of Culver City. The high court’s order of remand directed 
the Court of Appeal to reexamine its prior judgment “in 
light of Dolan v. City of Tigard (1994) 512 U.S. __,, 114 
S.Ct. 2309... . ” (Ehrlich v. City of Culver City (1994) 512 
U.S. __ [114 S.Ct. 2731].) 


Following remand, a divided Court of Appeal reaf- 
firmed its earlier ruling in favor of defendant city in an 
unpublished opinion. We then granted the petition for 
review by plaintiff, a property owner and developer, to 
consider important and unsettled questions concerning 
the extent to which the high court’s opinions in Dolan v. 
City of Tigard (1994) 512 U.S. ___ [114 S.Ct. 2309] (Dolan) 
and the earlier case of Nollan v. California Coastal Commis- 
sion (1987) 483 U.S. 825 (Nollan) apply to development 
permits that exact a fee as a condition of issuance, rather 


A2 


than, as in both Nollan and Dolan, the possessory dedica- 
tion of real property. 


As we explain, we conclude that the tests formulated 
by the high court in its Dolan and Nollan opinions for 
determining whether a compensable regulatory taking 
has occurred under the takings clause of the Fifth 
Amendment to the federal Constitution apply, under the 
circumstances of this case, to the monetary exaction imposed 
by Culver City as a condition of approving plaintiff's 
request that the real property in suit be rezoned to permit 
the construction of a multi-unit residential condominium. 
We thus reject the city’s contention that the heightened 
takings clause standard formulated by the court in Nollan 
and Dolan applies only to cases in which the local land 
use authority requires the developer to dedicate real 
property to public use as a condition of permit approval. 


We arrive at this conclusion not by reference to the 
constitutional takings clause alone, but within the statu- 
tory framework presented by the Mitigation Fee Act. 
(Gov. Code, section 66000 et seq.) We will conclude in this 
case that, in order to avoid substantial questions concern- 
ing the constitutional sufficiency of the legislative stan- 
dard embodied in the Act, the tests formulated by the 
high court in its Dolan and Nollan opinions for determin- 
ing when a regulatory taking has occurred apply here to 
the Act’s requirement that the local regulatory authority 
demonstrate a “reasonable relationship” between the 
monetary exaction and the public impact of the develop- 
ment. 


We thus interpret the Act’s “reasonable relationship” 
standard, as applied to the development fee at issue in 


in? eo eee 


CL shh Net APA A A tea ht al aed he gle Ls ee See ee ee 


A3 


this case, as embodying the standard of review formu- 
lated by the high court in its Nollan and Dolan opinions - 
proof by the local permitting authority of both an “essen- 
tial nexus” or relationship between the permit condition 
and the public impact of the proposed development, and 
of a “rough proportionality” between the magnitude of 
the fiscal exaction and the effects of the proposed devel- 


opment. 


Applying this standard in this case, we conclude, 
first, that the city has met its burden of demonstrating the 
required connection or nexus between the rezoning — to 
permit a residential use of a parcel of land zoned for 
private recreational use — and the imposition of a mone- 
tary exaction to be expended in support of recreational 
purposes as a means of mitigating that loss. We conclude, 
however, that the record before us is insufficient to sus- 
tain the city’s determination that plaintiff pay a so-called 
mitigation fee of $280,000 as a condition for approval of 
his request that the property be rezoned to permit the 
construction of a condominium project. Because the city 
may be able to justify the imposition of some fee under 
the recently minted standard of Dolan, we follow the 
Oregon Supreme Court's disposition in that case and 
direct that the cause be remanded to the city for addi- 
tional proceedings in accordance with this opinion.

Factual and Procedural Background 
A 


Between 1973 and 1975, plaintiff acquired a vacant 
2.4-acre lot on Overland Avenue in Culver City and 
obtained city approval to develop the site as a private 


A4 


tennis club and recreational facility. At plaintiff's request, 
the city amended its zoning and general plan ordinances 
governing uses on the property from a split zone R-1 
(single family residential) and C-2 (retail commercial) to 
C-3 (commercial). A specific plan was also adopted by the 
city providing for the development of a privately oper- 
ated tennis club and recreational facility.' A report pre- 
pared by city planning officials in 1974 recommending 
approval of the development permit recognized that “the 
need for additional tennis facilities in this city is a real 
one”; the planning commission resolution recommending 
approval likewise observed that “[t]he proposed zoning 
of the property in conjunction with the specific plan will 
provide a suitably located area within the City for addi- 
tional tennis club facilities in the form of a private tennis 
club.” From 1975 to 1988, plaintiff, alone or through 
others, operated the sports complex - consisting by then 
of a swimming pool, five tennis courts, racquetball 
courts, weight training and aerobic facilities — on the site. 


In 1981, in response to financial losses, plaintiff 
applied to the city for a change in land use in order to 
construct an office building on the site; that application 
was abandoned after the city planning commission rec- 
ommended against approval on the ground that the exis- 
ting sports and tennis club provided a needed 
commercial recreational facility within the city. The club 
continued in operation under a series of managers until 


' A “specific plan” implements and refines the general plan 
by allowing for greater specificity as to permissible uses. (Gov. 
Code, § 65450.) 


aa 


Pee ee ee Se Tes ‘

August 1988, when plaintiff closed it as a result of con- 
tinuing financial losses. The following month, he again 
applied to the city for an amendment to the general plan, 
a zoning change and amendment of the specific plan to 
allow construction of a 30-unit condominium complex 
valued at $10 million. 


Shortly after the submission of plaintiff's application, 
the city expressed an interest in acquiring the property 
for operation as a municipally owned sports facility and 
hired outside consultants to study the feasibility of the 
acquisition. The impetus behind the city’s interest was a 
perceived deficiency in existing municipal recreational 
facilities. Buying the property, according to a city council 
staff report, offered the “opportunity to preserve an exis- 
ting sports/recreational facility for public use and relieve 
pressure on existing facilities.” The feasibility study con- 
cluded that, by national standards, the city was two to 
four tennis courts short, and deficient in the number of its 
public swimming pools and gymnasiums. The study also 
concluded that plaintiff's club had encountered financial 
problems through a combination of management prob- 
lems, poor maintenance, and a lack of competitive ameni- 
ties offered by other clubs. Without extensive capital 
improvements, the study concluded, the club could not 
“compete financially in today’s health and fitness mar- 
ket.” 


Based upon the findings of the study, the city con- 
cluded that it lacked the funds to purchase and operate 
the club as a general public sports complex, and would 
incur substantial financial risks if it purchased and oper- 
ated the club on a limited membership, fee-for-service 
basis. In April 1989, the city decided not to purchase the 


A6 


property. At the same time, the city council disapproved 
plaintiff's application based on concerns over the loss of 
a recreational land use needed by the community. In the 
meantime, plaintiff obtained a demolition permit and tore 
down the existing site improvements. The still useful 
equipment, including the tennis court lights, nets, and 
lockers, he donated to the city. 


Following the rejection of his application, plaintiff 
entered into discussions with members of the city council 
and city staff in an attempt to restructure the project. He 
asserts that he was informed the project would not be 
approved unless he agreed to build new recreational facil- 
ities for the city. In response, plaintiff apparently raised 
the possibility of constructing four new municipal tennis 
courts. During this time period plaintiff filed, but did not 
serve, a petition for writ of mandate and complaint for 
damages commencing this lawsuit. Following a closed- 
door meeting ostensibly to discuss the pending litigation, 
the city council voted to approve plaintiff’s application 
conditioned upon the payment of certain monetary exac- 
tions. In lieu of the construction of four tennis courts as a 
condition of approval, the city required the payment of 
$280,000 “to be used” as stated in the ratifying ordinance, 
“for additional [public] recreational facilities as directed 
by the City Council.” The minutes of the city council 
meeting state that the $280,000 fee was to be used “for 
partial replacement of the lost recreational facilities .. . ” 
occasioned by the specific plan amendment. The amount 
of the fee was based upon a city study which showed that 
the replacement costs for the recreational facilities “lost” 
as a result of amending the specific plan would be 
$250,000 to $280,000 for the pool, $135,000 to 150,000 for

The Mitigation Fee Act (Gov. Code, § 66000 et seq.) 


As noted, this case arises within the statutory frame- 
work of the Mitigation Fee Act (the Act), introduced in 
the Legislature as Assembly Bill No. 1600, 1987-1988 Keg- 
ular Session, and enacted as Statutes 1987, chapter 927, 
effective January 1, 1989. The Act, codified as sections 
66000-66003 of the Government Code, sets forth pro- 
cedures for protesting the imposition of fees and other 
monetary exactions imposed on a development by a local 
agency. As its legislative history evinces, the Act was 
passed by the Legislature “in response to concerns among 
developers that local agencies were imposing develop- 
ment fees for purposes unrelated to development pro- 
jects.” (Centex Real Estate Corp. v. City of Vallejo (1993) 19 
Cal.App.4th 1358, 1361; Sen. Local Gov. Com. analysis of 
Assem. Bill No. 1600 (1987-1988 Reg. Sess.) p. 1; see also 
Garrick Development Co. v. Hayward Unified School Dist. 
(1992) 3 Cal.App.4th 320.) 


Plaintiff complied with the requirements of Govern- 
ment Code section 66020 by filing a protest with the city 
which enumerated all of the bases of his challenge to the 
recreational and art fees, including his constitutional tak- 
ings claim. These claims were subsequently set forth in 
the complaint and writ petition. In the subsequent agree- 
ment between plaintiff and the city, plaintiff agreeg [sic] 
to pay the disputed fees and to have a lien recorded 
against the property, and the city agreed to allow the 
project to proceed, and further stipulated that “[n]othing 
in this agreement shall in any way waive or restrict 
[plaintiff’s] rights to pursue the protest [plaintiff] has

a 
= 
: 
x

made under [former] Government Code § 66008 [now 
section 66020] and by the above-mentioned lawsuit.” 
Thus, the agreement expressly preserved both the statu- 
tory claim under the Act and the takings claims set forth 
in plaintiff's statutory protest and in his lawsuit.‘ 


Although for the most part procedural in nature, the 
Act also embodies a statutory standard against which 
monetary exactions by local governments subject to its 
provisions are measured. Government Code section 66001 
requires the local agency to determine “how there is a 
reasonable relationship” between the proposed use of a 
given exaction and both “the type of development pro- 
ject” and “the need for the public facility and the type of 
development project on which the fee is imposed.” (Gov. 
Code, § 66001, subd. (a)(3), (4), italics added.) In addition, 
the local agency must determine how there is a “reason- 
able relationship” between “the amount of the fee and the 
cost of the public facility or portion of the public facility 
attributable to the development on which the fee is 
imposed.” (Id., § 66001, subd. (b), italics added.) 


4 In its brief on the merits, the city has raised two 
additional issues. It asserts that plaintiff preserved only his right 
to challenge the exactions under the Act. This argument was not 
raised below or in a counter-petition for review; it is therefere 
not cognizable before this court. (Cal. Rules of Court, rule 
29(b)(1).) Furthermore, it is factually inaccurate. The city also 
asserts that the takings challenge is somehow not “ripe” 
because plaintiff waived all but his statutory challenge to the 
fees. The argument was not raised below and is therefore not 
cognizable before this court. Moreover, as noted above, it is also 
factually untenable. 


Al2 


The Act thus codifies, as the statutory standard appli- 
cable by definition to nonpossessory monetary exactions, 
the “reasonable relationship” standard employed in Cali- 
fornia and elsewhere to measure the validity of required 
dedications of land (or fees imposed in lieu of such 
dedications) that are challenged under the Fifth and 
Fourteenth Amendments. (See, e.g., Ayres v. City Council 
of Los Angeles (1949) 34 Cal.2d 31; Associated Home Builders 
etc., Inc. v. City of Walnut Creek (1971) 4 Cal.3d 633; Grupe 
v. California Coastal Commission (1985) 166 Cal.App.3d 148; 
cf. Nollan, supra, 483 U.S. at pp. 839-840; Dolan, supra, 512 
U.S. at p. __ [114 S.Ct. at p. 2319] [“Some form of the 
reasonable relationship test has been adopted in many 
. . . jurisdictions.” ].) 


As we explain, the high court’s opinions in Nollan 
and Dolan cast substantial doubt on the sufficiency of the 
Associated Homebuilders standard, at least as applied to 
cases such as this one, where the property owner chal- 
lenges an individualized exaction imposed as a condition 
of issuance of a development permit as an uncompen- 
sated taking under the Fifth Amendment. The high 
court’s recent takings jurisprudence, as we comprehend 
it, underlines the separate nature of the takings clause as 
an independent constitutional guarantee, one that is not 
only distinct from the commands of the due process and 
equal protection provisions of the federal constitution, 
but which embodies a standard of judicial review that is 
greater than the “minimal level of scrutiny” mandated by 
those provisions. (Dolan, supra, 512 U.S. ___ [114 S.Ct. at 
p- 2319].) 


We do not believe, however, that these conceptual 
obscurities need cause problems in practice. Although the
