DISCUSSION 
A. The $280.000 Exaction 


The City required Ehrlich to pay $280,000 as a condi- 
tion to obtaining a building permit to develop his prop- 
erty into 30 condominium units. We find this exaction 
impermissible under the law. 


The argument that the City needed the $280,000 fee 
to mitigate the loss of needed community recreational 
facilities is misplaced. The private facility of Ehrlich had 
been shut down prior to the determination that the City 
needed Ehrlich to pay a substantial sum of money to 
compensate the City for the loss of recreational facilities 
that were not being used by the community. In effect, 
what the City was attempting to do was to force Ehrlich 
to reopen a business that had lost money for approxi- 
mately 15 years under numerous managers. Certainly the 
government cannot force landowners to use their prop- 
erty for a particular type of business. (Department of Pub- 
lic Works v. San Diego (1932) 122 Cal.App. 159) In 
addition, the right not to be forced to use land for a 
particular business includes the right to go out of busi- 
ness. In Robinson v. Diamond Housing Corporation (D.C. 
Cir. 1972) 463 F.2d 853, the court stated as follows: “There 
would be severe constitutional problems with a rule of 
law which required an entrepreneur to remain in business 
against his will.” (Id. at p. 867.) We believe the City is in 
error when it requires the specific business that may be 
operated on Ehrlich’s land, or that it had a right to the 


Pr ener er gee 


G9 


continued use of Ehrlich’s land as a recreational facility 
even at a loss to its owner. 


In Agins v. City of Tiburon (Agins I) (1979) 24 Cal.3d 
266, 277, the court stated that a zoning ordinance may be 
unconstitutional and subject to invalidation only when its 
effect is to deprive the landowner of substantially all 
reasonable use of his property. In Agins v. Tiburon (Agins 
IT) (1980) 447 U.S. 255, 260-261, the Supreme Court held 
that the application of a general zoning law to particular 
property effects a taking if it denies an owner economi- 
cally viable use of his land. 


The facts are clear that Ehrlich was denied an eco- 
nomically viable use of his land without the zone change. 
Between the time of Ehrlich’s purchase of the property 
and its closure in 1988, the facility was operated by five 
different managers, without making the legally zoned 
business profitable. The City conducted a feasibility 
study and in the report dated March 29, 1989, determined 
that it was extremely doubtful that the facility could 
operate on a break-even or profitable basis without more 
indoor space and amenities. The report also stated that if 
the facility was upgraded and substantial money spent, it 
could make a profit if there were 5,000 members, signifi- 
cantly more members than ever belonged during 
Ehrlich’s 15 years of ownership. 


The City certainly could have taken Ehrlich’s prop- 
erty under eminent domain laws and paid Ehrlich fair 
compensation. The City could then have legitimately 
used the property for recreational purposes. However, it 
appears that the City attempted to exact an unfair price 


G10 


from one developer to help finance the City’s recreational 
facilities. 


The trial court relied on Nollan v. California Coastal 
Comm'n (1987) 483 U.S. 825 and Rohn v. City of Visalia 
(1989) 214 Cal.App.3d 1463 in determining that the 
$280,000 exaction was not valid. 


The case of Nollan v. California Coastal Comm'n, supra, 
483 U.S. 825 involved a permit application to demolish a 
small bungalow, totaling 504 square feet and replace it 
with a larger two-story house on a privately held ocean- 
front lot. The new three-bedroom house would be in 
keeping with the rest of the neighborhood. The coastal 
commission found that a new house would block public 
views of the ocean from an inland vantage point. The 
commission attempted to require the Nollans to dedicate 
an easement allowing public use of their private beach on 
the ocean side of their house. (Id. at p. 828.) 


The Supreme Court established a two-step nexus for 
land use exactions tied to government approvals. First, 
the Court requires that the circumstance of the applica- 
tion must be such that the government agency could deny 
the proposed request altogether without that denial being 
a taking. (Id. at p. 835.) In Nollan, the Court assumed, 
without deciding the issue, that this criteria was met 
under the facts (protecting public views was a legitimate 
justificatien for banning the Nollans’ project). Second, if 
the outright denial of the request would not be a taking, 
the government may approve the request with a condi- 
tion which directly furthers the goal that could have 
justified complete prohibition of the project. (Id. at p. 
836.) This nexus test states: “In short, unless the permit 


Gil 


condition serves the same governmental purpose as the 
development ban, the building restriction is not a valid 
regulation of land use but ‘an out-and-out plan of extor- 
tion.’ ” (Id. at p. 837.) 


In Nollan, the court found it “impossible to under- 
stand how a requirement that people already on the 
public beaches be able to walk across the Nollans’ prop- 
erty reduces any obstacles to viewing the beach created 
by the new house.” (Id. at p. 838.) 


In the case at bar, the City cannot pass either of the 
two parts of Nollan. We find no legitimate justification for 
banning Ehrlich’s condominium project. The argument 
that the project would deprive the City of needed recre- 
ational facilities is without merit. Ehrlich had closed his 
recreational facility and certainly the City could not have 
forced Ehrlich to reopen the facility to provide the City 
with much needed recreational facilities. The $280,000 
exaction was not to mitigate or reduce a harm caused by 
Ehrlich’s project. It was the City’s way to provide a 
public benefit at the expense of one individual. There 
certainly is the possibility that the 30 units would create a 
need for more recreational facilities. However, that is the 
reason that the City legally imposed the in-lieu parkland 
fee of $30,000. The in-lieu parkland fee was not objected 
to by Ehrlich and is certainly a legitimate exercise of 
authority by the city. The city cannot condition the build- 
ing permit for the project upon Ehrlich’s paying $280,000. 


The City cannot pass the second part of the Nollan 
test. There would not have been a governmental purpose 
served if the project would have been banned. The City 
certainly would not have had the continued operation of 


G12 


Ehrlich’s recreational facility since it had been out of 
business for approximately one year prior to the City 
approving the project. There appears to be no recogni- 
zable legitimate governmental purpose for precluding 
Ehrlich from developing the property. In fact, there is a 
strong likelihood that a substantial governmental interest 
would have been served by allowing the non-used land 
to provide housing for several families. In addition, it is 
quite possible that the aesthetics of the City would have 
been served by the project’s addition to the community. 
The $280,000 exaction certainly exceeds the reasonable 
cost of providing park and recreational activities for 30 
town homes. 


The City’s reliance on Gilbert v. State of California 
(1990) 218 Cal.App.3d 234 is misplaced. Gilbert follows 
Nollan in requiring the permit condition to serve the same 
governmental purpose as the development ban. In Gilbert, 
it was established that the water moratorium insured a 
potable water supply. The denial of the operating permit 
resulted in the same end, a potable water supply. The 
Nollan nexus was present. (Id. at pp. 253-254.) 


In Rohn v. City of Visalia, supra, 214 Cal.App.3d 1463, 
the City conditioned a zone change and building permit 
for an office building upon the dedication of 14 percent of 
Rohn’s land to be used to correct the street alignment at 
the intersection where the property was located. This 
condition was imposed even though the City had deter- 
mined that Rohn’s proposed office building would gener- 
ate less traffic impact than if the property were developed 
as multiple family dwellings as permitted by the then 
existing zoning. In Rohn, the court found that the pro- 
posed dedication bore no relationship to the use of 


G13 


Rohn’s property and the City could not impose it as a 
means of shifting the cost of a public benefit to another 
party. (Id. at pp. 1474-1476.) In Rohn, the street realign- 
ment was made necessary by an instance of poor plan- 
ning during the original development of the intersection, 
and the City viewed the landowner’s application for 
rezoning and site plan review as the “hook” it needed to 
acquire the property for nothing. It is quite possible that 
in the case at bar, the City viewed Ehrlich as the “hook” it 
needed to acquire needed recreational facilities at the 
expense of one developer. 


The record is certainly devoid of evidence showing 
why a 30-unit condominium project creates an impact on 
the City requiring $280,000 of public recreational fees 
above and beyond the $30,000 already required of Ehrlich 
under the in-lieu parkland fee.4 


B. Attorneys’ Fees Pursuant to Code of Civil Pro- 
cedure section 1036 


The City argues that even if the mitigation fee does 
not meet constitutional standards as a valid development 
exaction, the City did not take any interest in Ehrlich’s 
property. We disagree. The trial court decided that a 
taking had taken place under the test established in 
Nollan. In Nollan, the Supreme Court held that where a 
permit condition fails to substantially advance the origi- 
nal purpose of the building restriction, the condition 
becomes a plan to extort a public benefit without the 


* Since we find that the $280,000 fee is not justified 
pursuant to Nollan, we find it unnecessary to discuss the other 
arguments advanced by Ehrlich to invalidate the fee. 


G14 


payment of compensation in violation of the fifth amend- 
ment. It was the City’s unconstitutional imposition of the 
$280,000 exaction that constitutes the unlawful taking in 
this case. Attorney's fees and costs under Code of Civil 
Procedure section 1036 are mandated. 


We conclude that denial of all viable use of the prop- 
erty is not required to an award pursuant to Code of Civil 
Procedure section 1036. Section 1036 requires only a tak- 
ing of “any interest in real property.” Interpreting the 
predecessor statute, section 1246.3, the courts have recog- 
nized the broad interpretation of the term “taking.” In 
Holtz v. San Francisco Bay Area Rapid Transit Dist. (1976) 17 
Cal.3d. 648, 653, the court commented that the Legisla- 
ture intended to provide recovery for “encroachments 
upon lesser property interests.” Also in Holtz the court 
held that a “taking” as used in the statute is less restric- 
tive than a “taking” under California Constitution article 
I, section 19.5 


Cc. Arts in Public Places Exaction 


(1) Government Code Sections 66000 Et Seq. 


Ehrlich argues that the art in public places exaction 
must be vacated because the City failed to comply with 


5 Article I, section 19 provides as follows: “Private property 
may be taken or damaged for public use only when just 
compensation, ascertained by a jury unless waived, has first 
been paid to, or into court for, the owner. The Legislature may 
provide for possession by the condemnor following 
commencement of eminent domain proceedings upon deposit in 
court and prompt release to the owner of money determined by 
the court to be the probable amount of just compensation.” 


G15 


the requirements of Government Code sections 66000 et 
seq. We disagree. Government Code sections 66000-66009 
govern fees for development projects. However, we con- 
clude the provisions of section 66000 et seq. do not apply 
to the City’s art in public places program. 


The developer's fee statute applies to local govern- 
ment actions which impose a fee as a condition of 
approval after January 1, 1989. The art in public places 
program allocation is not a fee as defined in the devel- 
oper’s fee statute. Government Code section 66000, sub- 
division (b) provides in part: “ ‘Fee’ means a monetary 
exaction, other than a tax or special assessment, which is 
charged by a local agency to the applicant in connection 
with approval of a development project for the purpose 
of defraying all or a portion of the cost of public facilities 
related to the development project. . . .” The language is 
clear that a developer fee involves a monetary exaction. 
That is not the case in the City’s arts in public places 
exaction, The program allows the developer the option of 
placing art on the project site, or at the developer’s 
option, paying a fee in lieu of placing the art work on the 
project. The trial court correctly used the analogy to a 
landscaping requirement for a project. It would appear 
reasonable that if a City could impose a requirement of 
landscaping on a project, the City could also impose a 
requirement of some sort of art to be placed on the 
project. Ehrlich elected not to place the art on the project 
and cannot now complain concerning the monetary exac- 
tion the City imposed pursuant to its art in public places 
ordinance. 


G16 


The developer's fees statutes expressly excludes the 
in-lieu parkland fees which may be imposed on a devel- 
opment pursuant to Government Code section 66477. The 
in-lieu art fee is very similar to an in-lieu parkland fee. 
Under either an in-lieu parkland fee or an in-lieu art fee, 
the fees are only paid upon the developer’s election. The 
trial court concluded that Ehrlich elected to pay the fee in 
lieu of placement of art. 


We do not believe that the art in public places pro- 
gram results in a possessory taking of land. The art 
program did not require any public dedication of land. 
According to City’s ordinance section 33H-31(a) any art 
work installed by the property owner belongs to the 
property owner. 


(2) Legitimate Exercise of the Police Power of 
City 

There are legitimate public interests in the pursuit of 

art and artistic creations. In 1976, the Legislature adopted 
the “Art in Public Buildings Program.”® In addition, in 
1979 and in 1982, the Legislature enacted statutes 
designed to preserve works of art by the adoption of Civil 
Code sections 987 and 989. The adoption of the art in 
public places program by the City in 1988 was certainly 


6 Government Code section 15813 provides as follows: 
“The Legislature finds and declares that the State of California 
has a responsibility for expanding public experience with art. 
The Legislature recognizes that other states have enacted 
legislation requiring the expenditure of 1 percent of funds 
allocated for the construction of state buildings for works of art 
for such buildings.” 


G17 


the type of program envisioned by the Legislature and 
was certainly within the legitimate police powers of the 
City. Even in Nollan, supra, 483 U.S. at p. 835, Justice 
Scalia, reaffirmed that there is a wide range of legitimate 
government interests. 


It is not our duty to look at the wisdom of the City 
when it enacted the arts in public place ordinance or to 
determine if the providing of art in an urban environment 
will enhance the quality of life, health, or welfare. In 
Garrick Development Co. v. Hayward Unified School Dist. 
(1992) 3 Cal.App.4th 320, the court upheld the imposition 
of school fees pursuant to Government Code sections 
53080 and 65995. In Garrick, there was a criticism of using 
permanent construction as opposed to less costly, mov- 
able structures. However, the court said the choice 
between the two was one for the school district and their 
wisdom was not to be second-guessed. (Id. at p. 333.) 
While we may believe that there may be other ways to 
fulfill the purpose of the arts in-lieu ordinance, it passes 
constitutional muster and our inquiry stops there. 


Ehrlich’s argument concerning the one percent 
allocation of the ordinance is without merit. This cost is 
consistent with the Legislature’s recognition of the one 
percent in the Art in Public Buildings Program. (Gov. 
Code, § 15813.) This certainly appears to be a reasonable 
figure for the developer if the developer elects not to 
place art work on the property himself. One percent is 
certainly substantially less than is frequently required for 
landscaping. 


G18 


(3) Special Tax 


Ehrlich also argues that the art in public places pro- 
gram is a special tax imposed without a two-thirds vote 
of the electorate. We disagree. The arts in public places 
ordinance is not a way for the City to replace revenue lost 
as a result of Proposition 13. There was no revenue pro- 
ducing function by the City’s art in public place program. 
All developments that are subject to the program have 
the choice of placing artwork on the property and they 
will be the owners of the artwork. If all developers were 
to choose this option, it could hardly be argued that the 
program would be a special tax and prohibited without 
the vote of two-thirds of the citizens. We believe that if 
the City would have wanted the program to be revenue 
producing, it would not have been designed as a program 
that provided the in-lieu option. 


As the City pointed out correctly in their brief, the 
City’s art in public places allocation is related to the 
increased needs created by the condominiums and is 
similar to the rationale underlying the park dedication 
for subdivisions sustained in Associated Home Builders 
Etc., Inc. v. City of Walnut Creek (1971) 4 Cal.3d 633.7 


D. Attorney’s Fees 


Ehrlich requested an award of attorney’s fees pur- 
suant to Government Code section 800 and Code of Civil 


?7 We also reject Ehrlich’s argument that the ordinance 
violates his free speech. The developer determines the art work 
to be used, subject to very limited input by the City. 


G19 


Procedure section 1021.5. The trial court denied Ehrlich’s 
request and found that “[t]hese issues are not so clear as 
to justify imposition of fees.” We do not find an abuse of 
discretion in the denying of attorney's fees pursuant to 
these sections. 


In Kreutzer v. County of San Diego (1984) 153 
Cal.App.3d 62, 78, the court held that the determination 
of whether an action is arbitrary or capricious is essen- 
tially one of fact and is within the sound discretion of the 
trial court. In Forrest-v. Trustees of Cal. State University & 
Colleges (1984) 160 Cal.App.3d 357, 368, the court deter- 
mined that the question of arbitrary or capricious is a 
question of fact and the decision of the court will be 
sustained on appeal unless an abuse of discretion is 
shown. 


The decision as to whether attorney’s fees are war- 
ranted pursuant to Code of Civil Procedure section 1021.5 
rests within the sound discretion of the trial court. (Bag- 
gett v. Gates (1982) 32 Cal.3d 128, 142.) There was a proper 
basis for the trial court to deny attorney’s fees to Ehrlich. 
While Ehrlich was certainly the prevailing party as to the 
$280,000 mitigation fee, the trial court still did not grant 
attorney’s fees under either section. Under Code of Civil 
Procedure section 1021.5, the denial was proper because 
the trial court may not award attorney’s fees if there is a 
Significant private benefit, as opposed to a public one. 
Certainly Ehrlich’s purpose in fighting the $280,000 fee 
was for his own pecuniary interest and the Code of Civil 
Procedure section was not designed to reward litigants 
motivated by their own pecuniary interest. (Beach Colony 
II v. California Coastal Com. (1985) 166 Cal.App.3d 106, 
113-115.) 


G20 


E. Reinstatement of the Lien 


City filed their notice of appeal and deposit of release 
of lien pending decision of the Court of Appeal. The City 
argues that with the filing of that notice of appeal and 
deposit of release of lien, jurisdiction over those issues 
determined by the court on December 14, 1990 was 
removed to the Court of Appeal. We disagree. 


The law provides that the trial court may provide 
that its order granting a writ of mandate is not stayed 
pending appeal. 


The superior court found irreparable damage.? The 
City incorrectly relies on Code of Civil Procedure section 


8 Code of Civil Procedure section 1110b provides as 
follows: “If an appeal be taken from an order or judgment 
granting a writ of mandate the court granting the writ, or the 
appellate court, may direct that the appeal shall not operate as a 
stay of execution if it is satisfied upon the showing made by the 
petitioner that he will suffer irreparable damage in his business 
or profession if the execution is stayed.” 


° The minute order of the court dated December 12, 1990 
stated: “The judgment requires release of the lien imposed to 
secure the tennis court exaction. That provision of the judgment 
is appropriate inasmuch as the exaction, and hence the lien, has 
been ruled invalid after trial. The judgment will be modified, 
however, to provide that the lien securing the tennis court 
exaction must be removed no later than 1/7/90 [1/7/91] in 
order to allow time for the City to seek appellate court action on 
this point before the lien is removed. If, after removal of the lien, 
the tennis court exaction is reimposed by the appellate courts, 
the City can still collect the amount imposed from petitioner; no 
indication to the contrary appears. The amount of the exaction 
should not constitute a lien on the condominiums which by that 
time will likely be owned by third party purchasers. Nor should 
the City be able to thwart success of the project by frustrating 


G21 


917.3. Section 917.3 only applies “if the judgment or order 
appealed from directs the execution of one or more 
instruments. .. . ” The minute order or the partial judg- 
ment or the writ did not direct the execution of any 
particular instrument, just the removal of the lien by 
January 7, 1991. 


The revised partial judgment was not stayed by the 
notice of appeal filed by the City and the trial court 
properly ordered release of the lien. 


DISPOSITION 


The judgment is affirmed. Each party to pay their 
own costs.

has been ruled, after trial, invalid.”

(Parties and counsel checked if present) 
C 730 079 Counsel for 
Richard K. Ehrlich, etc., Plaintiff 
vs Counsel for 
City of Culver City, et al., Defendant 


NATURE OF PROCEEDINGS. 


PETITION FOR WRIT OF MANDATE 


In this matter heretofore submitted on August 14, 
1990, the court announces its decision as follows: 


I. The Tennis Court ($280,000) Exaction: 


1. Petitioner is building a condominium project 
which will presumably bring additional residents to the 
area. Petitioner may therefore be assessed with an in-lieu 
parkland fee - this seems not to be disputed. See also, 


OO EEE | 


H2 


regarding parkland exactions, Associated Homebuilders v. 
City of Walnut Creek, 4 C3 630 (1971). 


2. Important here is that it be clear that in issue here 
is not an in-lieu parkland fee, but rather an additional fee 
above the in-lieu parkland fee. The stated purpose of this 
additional fee is to “replace” tennis courts no longer 
available in the community due to the closing of peti- 
tioner’s private tennis club. 


3. Petitioner’s club (although most likely a public 
accommodation for civil rights purposes) was at all times 
private property; the city never owned any interest in it 
nor was any part of it ever dedicated to public use. It is 
conceded that petitioner had no obligation to maintain 
his facility available for public use, but rather that peti- 
tioner retained the right to exclude the public from his 
premises. It is true that the public accommodation laws 
would essentially require petitioner to make the facilities 
available to the public generally so long as the premises 
were operated as a business, but there was no require- 
ment that petitioner operate the premises as a business. 
Instead, petitioner was at all times free to go out of 
business, which he in fact did. 


4. This situation is controlled by Nollan v. Coastal 
Commission and Rohn v. City of Visalia. In Rohn, a Califor- 
nia appellate case following Nollan, the court noted: 


“4 


- A grant of public privilege, such as a 
building permit, may not be conditioned upon 
the deprivation of constitutional protections 
. . . An arbitrarily conceived exaction will be 
nullified as a disguised attempt to take private 
property for public use without resort to emi- 
nent domain or as a mask for discriminatory 


H3 


taxation .. . If the applicant must donate prop- 
erty for a public use that bears no relationship 
to the benefit conferred on the applicant or the 
burden imposed on the public, there is a taking 
of property... ” 


a 


. . Where the conditions imposed are not 
reasonably related to the landowner’s proposed 
use, but are imposed by the public entity to shift 
the burden of providing the cost of a public 
benefit to one not responsible, or only remotely 
or speculatively benefitting from it, there is an 
unreasonable exercise of police power.” 


5. Here, the property owner’s action in developing 
a condominium project will likely create a need for more 
public recreation facilities in the area, but that is already 
addressed by the in-lieu parkland fee. There is otherwise 
no reasonable relation, within the requirements of Nollan 
and Rohn, between the petitioner’s project and the need 
for public tennis courts in Culver City. 


6. In Nollan, the issuance of the permit allowed 
construction of a house which would impair the public 
view of the public beach. Conditions designed to main- 
tain the view or to mitigate its obstruction were therefore 
found permissible. Conditions unrelated to the obstruc- 
tion of the view, in particular the requirement of the 
granting of a lateral easement along the beach and unre- 
lated to the view, were not permissible. 


7. The exaction of money instead of land is different 
in kind but not principle. In this case, petitioner’s actions 
cannot legally be said to deprive the City of tennis courts, 
because neither did petitioner have an affirmative duty to 
provide tennis courts to the City or its residents nor 


————————— 


H4 


would tennis courts necessarily be available to the City 
but for petitioner’s project (petitioner was at all times 
free to discontinue business as a tennis club, and in fact 
did so). The City’s proposed exaction is - as discussed in 
Rohn — simply an effort to shift the cost of providing a 
public benefit to one no more responsible for the need 
than any other taxpayer. 


8. The City could have condemned a portion of 
petitioner’s property for use as City tennis courts, but the 
City would then of course have had to pay for the land. 
Here, instead of taking land for which it would have had 
to pay, the City proposes to take not land but money. This 
is equally impermissible. 


9. Petition for writ of mandate granted as to the 
$280,000 tennis court exaction on grounds (b), (c) in the 
sense that the exaction constitutes an unequal tax dis- 
criminatorily applied, and that portion of (e) stating that 
the exaction constitutes a taking of private property with- 
out compensation. Petitioner to prepare order and judg- 
ment consistent with this order with return date in 
approximately 60 days. 


Il. The “Art in Public Places” Exaction: 


1. The City’s police powers extend to imposing cer- 
tain requirements on construction projects. For example, 
so long as not to the extent of a taking, the City may 
specify pursuant to its police powers setbacks, amount of 
open space to be retained, landscaping requirements, etc. 
Here the City has imposed a requirement of art place- 
ment or, in lieu of art placement, a fee for art at a 


H5 


different location. Petitioner has opted for the fee pay- 
ment. This seems quite analogous to an in-lieu parkland 
fee. 


2. If the City can require landscaping or the plant- 
ing of a tree on-site, could the City not allow instead as 
an option the funding of the planting of a tree or land- 
scaping off-site? It seems that for petitioner to invalidate 
the in-lieu art fee, petitioner would have to establish that 
the City has no power to require art on the site. If it can 
require art on the site, then by analogy to the parkland 
fee, the City can instead allow payment of a fee for art off 
the site. 


3. The court does not reach the issue of the City’s 
demand for an easement for viewing of art placed on site, 
since the on-site art option is not the option involved 
here. It if were, the easement portion of the requirement 
might be invalidated without invalidating the require- 
ment for placement of art visible from off-site. 


4. The court finds the art fee within the scope of the 
City’s police powers and the petition for writ of mandate 
invalidating the art exaction is therefore denied. Peti- 
tioner may set a further hearing, if petitioner so desires, 
regarding the question of the City’s compliance with 
proper procedure in specifying the use to which peti- 
tioner’s art fee shall be put and the steps taken to ensure 
that proper use. 


III. Attorney’s Fees: 


Attorney’s fees are denied. These issues are not so 
clear as to justify imposition of fees.

date to counsel for all appearing parties as shown:

RESPONDENT'S PETITION FOR REHEARING

INTRODUCTION 


Respondent and Cross-Appellant Richard K. Ehrlich 
(“Ehrlich”) seeks rehearing of the Court's opinion on 
rehearing filed May 25, 1993 and reinstatement of the 
Court’s opinion filed January 25, 1993. This Petition is 
brought pursuant to California Rule of Court 29. The 
bases for this Petition are as follows: 


1. Misstatements of fact regarding the imposition of 
the original land use regulations on the site; and 


2. Omissions and misstatements of fact regarding 
the economic and environmental viability of recreational 
facilities at the subject site. 


Specifically, the Court misunderstands the origin, 
nature and effect of the Specific Plan which the City 
imposed on the subject site. As a result, the Court has 
reached incorrect factual conclusions which are central to 
the Court’s opinion. When the true facts are known, the 
rationale of the Court’s opinion in this case disappears. 
Ehrlich respectfully submits, that absent these misunder- 
Standings of fact, the Court would not have changed its 
first ruling in this case affirming the trial court’s decision 
invalidating the $280,000.00 exaction. 


DISCUSSION 
I. 


THE $280,000 WAS NOT TO MITIGATE THE 
LOSS OF NEEDED RECREATION FACILITIES 


The rationale of the Court's opinion rests upon the 
City’s contention that the $280,000 was required to pro- 
mote the purpose of the Specific Plan, ie. to insure the 


Ce 


13 


existence and/or mitigate the loss of needed recreational 
facilities. The Specific Plan, however, was not adopted by 
the City to insure that City would be provided with 
needed recreational facilities, but rather to assuage sur- 
rounding neighbors who were vehemently opposed to 
the use of the site for any type of recreational facility, and 
to “mitigate any adverse effects which might otherwise occur” 
due to the construction of Ehrlich’s recreational facility on the 
site and to insure that no other C-3 uses, including recre- 
ational facilities other than Ehrlich’s recreational facility 
would be constructed and/or operated on the site. C.T. 497, 
486, 491, 502, 499-513. 


Consequently, the purpose of the Specific Plan is not 
promoted by the imposition of a “mitigation fee” to offset 
the loss of needed recreational facilities in the City. In 
truth, all of the arguments validating the $280,000.00 
proffered by the City are mere subterfuge to mask the 
City’s true intent to extort from Ehrlich a portion of the 
estimated $10,000,000 value of the townhome project. 


Il. 
THE SPECIFIC PLAN CONSTITUTES A TAKING 


The Court’s opinion appears to express an assump- 

tion that the Specific rian permitted Ehrlich to make 
alterations to his facility which might have permitted the 
profitable operation of some recreational facility on the 
site. The Specific Plan, however, prohibits the alteration 
of the recreational facility in any way. C.T. 497, 486, 491, 
502, 499-513. 


14 


By its own terms, the Specific Plan does not permit 
the operation of any recreational facility on the subject 
site. To the contrary, development and operation of a recre- 
ational facility was limited to the exact facility which Ehrlich 
and countless others had attempted to operate on the site for 12 
years. C.T. 485-513. 


By incorporating the site plan of the existing recre- 
ational facility into the Specific Plan, the Specific Plan, in 
effect, dictates the exact nature, including size, place- 
ment, type and location, of each improvement down to 
the last tree. Changes to Ehrlich’s facility were expressly 
prohibited. Any alteration of the improvements without 
amendment or removal of the Specific Plan would be 
unlawful and result in closing of the facility as well as 
other civil and/or criminal penalties. 


In its first ruling, the Court correctly stated that the 
Specific Plan and the City’s refusal to remove its limita- 
tions denied Ehrlich of all economically viable use of the 
subject site and constituted a taking. In support of this 
conclusion, this Court cited the findings contained in the 
feasibility study commissioned by the City to wit: It was 
extremely doubtful that the facility could operate at on a break- 
even or profitable basis in accordance with the layout mandated 
by the Specific Plan. A viable recreational facility would 
require substantial changes to the building structure and lay- 
out of the facility, including removal of various tennis courts, 
destruction of the existing building, and addition of at least 50 
parking spaces requiring acquisition of space from neighboring 
properties, all at a substantial expense. C.T. 1136-1143, 1147, 
1152, 1154-1156, 1166-1167, 1196-1199. The “circum- 
stances” cited in the record which might provide for the 
profitable operation of a recreational facility on the site 


I5 


are therefore not available in this case. Consequently, the 
Specific Plan denies Ehrlich all economically beneficial 
use of his property. 


Il. 


THE SPECIFIC PLAN LIMITATIONS DID NOT EXIST AT 
THE TIME EHRLICH PURCHASED THE PROPERTY 


In its Petition for Rehearing, the City has knowingly 
misled this Court by intentionally omitting material facts 
in order to misrepresent evidence contained in the record. 
The City is well aware that in September 1973, Ehrlich as 
president of and on behalf of Downtown Development 
Corporation (“DDC”) entered into an agreement to pur- 
chase the subject site from Mary Jane Brown aka Mary 
Jane Malone, Shiela Ann Malone, Anna Marie Rehm and 
Evelyn K. Carrington. Subsequent thereto, in early 1974, 
in connection with the development of Ehrlich’s recre- 
ational facility and on behalf of DDC, Ehrlich submitted a 
site plan to the City for approval. The City has this 
application in its files and is well aware that Ehrlich was 
acting on behalf of DDC. C.T. 485-495. Ehrlich appeared 
at numerous planning commission and City council meet- 
ings to answer questions and concerns of neighboring 
homeowners who vehemently opposed the use of the site 
as a recreational facility of any kind. C.T. 496, 499. The 
purchase of the subject property was finalized in January, 
1975, with title being vested in Ehrlich rather than DDC, 
one month prior to the February 1975 Specific Plan 
Amendment. C.T. 160, 510-512. 


The Specific Plan was not part of the bundle of 
“investment backed expectations” which Ehrlich and 


16 


DDC purchased when it entered into the purchase agree- 
ment in 1973. The Specific Plan was adopted in August 
1974 by the City Council in lieu of the usual and custom- 
ary procedure of site plan review. (The site plan review 
process does not impose by ordinance permanent limita- 
tions on the use of property.) The Specific Plan prohibited 
the use of the site for anything but Ehrlich’s recreational 
facility. C.T. 486. 


This is exactly the kind of over-restrictive and abu- 
sive zoning of which Justice Scalia warns in Nollan v. 
California Coastal Commission (1987) 483 U.S. 825, 837, 107 
S.Ct. 3141, 97 L.Ed.2d 677; 


“One would expect that a regime in which this 
kind of leveraging of the police power is 
allowed would produce stringent land-use regu- 
lations which the state then waives to accom- 
plish other purposes, leading to lesser 
realization of the land use goals purportedly 
sought to be served than would result from 
more lenient (but non-tradeable) development 
restrictions .. . ” 


The scheme adopted by the City in this case is exactly 
identical to that described by Justice Scalia. The Court's 
opinion permits and encourages a City to over regulate 
Property and then exact extortion monies from devel- 
opers to buy their way out of the overweening restric- 
tions. This is especially true if the Court permits the City 
to concoct, ex post facto, justifications for legislation 
which did not originally exist. If this is allowed, then 
Nollan no longer exists. 


17 


IV. 
CONCLUSION 


For all the foregoing reasons, Ehrlich respectfully 
requests that a rehearing be granted and that the opinion 
be modified to affirm the trial court’s holding invalidat- 
ing the $280,000.00 and awarding attorneys, fees pur- 
suant to Section 1036. 


Dated: June 8, 1993 By: /s/ Lisa S. Ehrlich 
LISA S. EHRLICH 
Attorney for Respondent 


jl

APPENDIX N 
ORDINANCE NO. 88-019 


AN ORDINANCE OF THE CITY OF CULVER CITY, CAL- 
IFORNIA, ADDING ARTICLE II TO CHAPTER 33H 
(COMMENCING WITH SECTION 33H-20) TO ESTAB- 
LISH AN ART IN PUBLIC PLACES PROGRAM, AND 
RESERVING SECTIONS 33H-12 THROUGH 33H-19, 
INCLUSIVE, FOR FUTURE USE. 


The City Council of the City of Culver City, Califor- 
nia, DOES HEREBY ORDAIN as follows: 


SECTION 1. Sections 33H-12 through 33H-19, inclu- 
sive, of the Culver City Municipal Code are hereby 
reserved for future use. 


SECTION 2. Article II is hereby added to Chapter 
33H to read: 


ARTICLE II 
ART IN PUBLIC PLACES 
Section 33H-20. Purpose. 


The City Council finds and declares: 


(a) Cultural and artistic resources enhance 
the quality of life for individuals living in 
working in and visiting the City. 


, 


(b) Balanced development of cultural and 
artistic resources preserves and improves the 
quality of the urban environment and increases 
real property values. 


(c) As development and revitalization of 
the real property within the City continues, the 
opportunity for creation of cultural and artistic 
resources is diminished. 


N2 


(d) As this development and revitalization 
continues as a result of market forces, urbaniza- 
tion of the community results. 


(e) As these opportunities are diminished 
and this urbanization occurs, the need to 
develop alternative sources for cultural and 
artistic outlets to improve the environment, 
image and character of the community is 
increased. 


(f) Development of cultural and artistic 
assets should be financed by those whose devel- 
opment and revitalization diminishes the avail- 
ability of the community’s resources for those 
opportunities and contributes to community 
urbanization. 


(g) Establishment of this Art in Public 
Places Program will promote the general welfare 
through balancing the community’s physical 
growth and revitalization and its cultural and 
artistic resources. 


Section 33H-21. Culver City Arts Committee Estab- 
lished. 


There shall be a Culver City Arts Committee to fulfill 
the duties established in this Article. The City Council 
may prescribe, by resolution, other duties, and the quali- 
fications of and appointment process for the members of 
the Committee. 


Section 33H-22. Fund Created. 


(a) There is hereby created a fund to be 
known as the “City Art Fund” to account for 
fees paid pursuant to this Article. This fund 
shall be maintained by the City Treasurer, and 


N3 


shall be used solely for the acquisition, installa- 
tion, improvement, maintenance and insurance 
of art work to be displayed in the City. 


(b) For a period of five years from the 
effective date of this Article, only expenditures 
for physical art as defined in Section 33H-30(a) 
shall be permitted. 


Section 33H-23. Applicability. 


(a) All new residential development of 
more than four units, and all commercial, indus- 
trial, and public building development projects, 
with a building valuation exceeding $500,000.00 
shall be subject to the provisions of this Article, 
provided that the value of a residential unit 
covenanted for low- or moderate-income house- 
holds, or for senior citizens shall not be 
included when determining the value of a resi- 
dential development. 


(b) Including but not limited to exterior 
and interior modifications and additions, all 
remodelling [sic] of existing residential build- 
ings of more than four units, and all remodell- 
ing [sic] of existing commercial, industrial and 
public buildings, shall be subject to the provi- 
sions of this Article when such remodelling [sic] 
has a valuation exceeding $250,000.00, excluding 
earthquake rehabilitation required by this Code 
for seismic safety. As used herein, the value of a 
residential unit covenanted for low- or mode- 
rate-income households or for senior citizens 
shall not be included when determining the 
value of a residential development. 


N4 


Section 33H-24. Certificates of Occupancy. 


(a) No final City approval, such as final 
inspection or a Certificate of Occupancy, for any 
project subject to this Article shall be granted or 
issued unless and until full compliance with the 
Art in Public Places Program is achieved, in one 
or more of the following ways: 


(1) The approved art work has been 
placed in a matter satisfactory to the Com- 
munity Development Director. 


(2) In lieu art fees have been paid. 


(3) Financial security in an amount 
equal to the acquisition and installation 
costs of an approved art work, in a form 
approved by the City Attorney, has been 
posted. 


(4) Donation of an approved art work 
has been accepted by the City Council. 


(b) For the purposes of this subsection (a), 
“full compliance with the Art in Public Places 
Program” shall not be found until the entire 
program allocation required by Section 33H-25 
for the project has been satisfied. 


(c) If any approved art work placed on 
private property pursuant to this Article is 
removed without City approval, the Certificate 
of Occupancy may be revoked. 


Section 33H-25. Program Allocations. 


(a) The program allocation, as used in this 
Article, is the percentage of the building cost 
which is set aside for the City’s Art in Public 
Places Program and shall be an amount equal to 


ieneneenemenemenentiesieesiiiiniaiiiaiiiiiiitiaiaiidiuiiiiaiiiaiauaill 


N5 


one percent (1%) of the total building valuation 
for an applicable project, excluding land acquisi- 
tion and off-site improvement costs. The total 
building valuation shall be computed using the 
latest Building Valuation Data as set forth by the 
International Conference of Building Officials 
(ICBO) unless, in the opinion of the Building 
Official, a different valuation measure should be 
used. 


(b) In lieu of placement of an approved art 
work, the applicant may pay to the City Art 
Fund an amount equal to the program allocation 
set forth in subdivision (a). 


(c) Nothing in this section shall prohibit 
the applicant from placing an approved art 
work with acquisition and installation costs in 
an amount less than the program allocations; 
provided that the applicant shall also pay to the 
City Art Fund an amount equal to the difference 
between the program allocation and the costs of 
acquisition and installation of such art work. 


Section 33H-26. Application Procedures for Place- 
ment of Art Work on Private Prop- 
erty. 


An application for placement of art work on private 
property shall be submitted to the Planning Division and 
shall include: 


(a) Preliminary sketches, photographs or 
other documentation of sufficient descriptive 
clarity to indicate the nature of the proposed art 
work; 


(b) An appraisal or other evidence of the 
value of the proposed art work, including acqui- 
sition and installation costs; 


N6 


(c) Preliminary plans containing such 
detailed information as may be required by the 
City Planner to adequately evaluate the location 
of the art work in relation to the proposed 
development, and its compatibility with the pro- 
posed development, including compatibility 
with the character of adjacent conforming devel- 
oped parcels and existing neighborhood if nec- 
essary to evaluate the proposal; and 


(d) A narrative statement to be submitted 
to the Culver City Arts Committee to demon- 
strate that the art work will be displayed in an 
area open and freely available to the general 
public at least ten hours each day, or otherwise 
provide public accessibility in an equivalent 
manner based on the characteristics of the art 
work or its placement on the site. 


Section 33H-27. Approval for Placement of 
Work on Private Property. 


(a) Except as provided in subsection (b), 
completed applications shall be submitted to the 
Culver City Arts Committee for review and 
approval of the art work, considering the aes- 
thetic quality and harmony of the art work with 
the existing on-site improvements, and the pro- 
posed location of and public accessibility to the 
art work. 


(b) If a development project is otherwise 
subject to Planning Commission review and 
approval and includes on-site placement of art 
work, the Culver City Arts Committee shall 
review the completed application and make a 
recommendation to the Planning Commission 
concerning the proposed art work and its pro- 
posed location, considering the aesthetic quality 
and harmony with the proposed project, and the 


Art 


N7 


public accessibility to the art work, including 
any recommended conditions of approval. The 
following shall apply to the review and 
approval of such development project and art 
work: 


(1) The Planning Commission shall con- 
sider the recommendations of the Culver 
City Arts Committee in its review and 
approval of the proposed project. 


(2) If the applicant proposes or the Plan- 
ning Commission recommends significant 
revisions to the architecture or physical 
design and layout of the proposed project 
subsequent to receipt of the Culver City 
Arts Committee recommendation, the 
revised application shall be returned to the 
Committee for further review and recom- 
mendation concerning the revised proposal 
prior to resubmittal to the Planning Com- 
mission for final review and approval. 


(c) Any person may seek review of a 
Culver City Arts Committee decision made pur- 
suant to subsection (a) by filing an appeal in 
accordance with Article XXA of Chapter 37 of 
this Code, commencing with Section 37-120A; 
provided that, for purposes of this section the 
reference herein to Planning Commission and 
Commissioners shall be deemed to mean Arts 
Committee and Committee members, respec- 
tively. 


Section 33H-28. Application Procedure for Accep- 
tance of Art Work Donated to the 
City. 


An application for acceptance of art work to be 
donated to the City shall include: 


N8 


(a) Preliminary sketches, photographs, 
models or other documentation of sufficient 
descriptive clarity to indicate the nature of the 
proposed art work; 


(b) An appraisal or other evidence of the 
value of the proposed art work, including acqui- 
sition and installation costs; 


(c) A written agreement executed by or on 
behalf of the artist who created the art work 
which expressly waives his or her rights under 
the California Art Preservation Act or other 
applicable law; and 


(d) Other information as may be required 
by the Culver City Arts Committee to ade- 
quately evaluate the proposed donation of art 
work. 


Section 33H-29. Review of Application for Accep- 
tance of Art Work Donated to the 
City. 


(a) Completed applications shall be sub- 
mitted to the Culver City Arts Committee for 
review and recommendation to the City Coun- 
cil. 


(b) Recommendations shall be forwarded 
to the City Council, which shall have the sole 
authority to accept or reject or conditionally 
accept the donation. 


Section 33H-30. Acceptable Art Work to be Limited. 


(a) For a period of five years from Septem- 
ber 29, 1988, only applications for approval or 
donation of art work in the following shall be 
processed: sculpture; murals or portable paint- 
ings; earthworks; fiberworks; neon; glass 


SE ee ee 


N9 


mosaics; photographs; prints; calligraphy; and 
other forms of physical hard media. 


(b) At the end of the first five years of the 
Art in Public Places Program, the Culver City 
Arts Committee shall review the limitations set 
forth in subsection (a) and shall recommend any 
proposed revisions to the City Council. 


Section 33H-31. Ownership of Art Work. 


(a) All art work placed on the site of an 
applicant’s project shall remain the property of 
the applicant; the obligation to provide all main- 
tenance necessary to preserve the art work in 
good condition shall remain with the owner of 
the site. 


(b) Maintenance of art work, as used in 
this Article, shall include without limitation, 
preservation of the art work in good condition 
to the satisfaction of the City, protection of the 
art work against physical defacement, mutila- 
tion or alteration, and securing and maintaining 
fire and extended coverage insurance and van- 
dalism coverage in an amount to be determined 
by the City Attorney. Prior to placement of an 
approved art work, applicant and owner of the 
site shall execute and record a covenant in a 
form approved by the City for maintenance of 
the art work. Failure to maintain the art work as 
provided herein is hereby declared to be a pub- 
lic nuisance. 


(c) In addition to all other remedies pro- 
vided by law, in the event the owner fails to 
maintain the art work, upon reasonable notice, 
the City may perform all necessary repairs, 
maintenance or secure insurance, and the costs 


N10 


therefor shall become a lien against the real 
property. 


(d) All art work donated to the City shall 
become the property of the City upon accep- 
tance by the City Council. 


Section 33H-32. Art Work on Public Property. 


(a) The Culver City Arts Committee shall 
prepare an annual plan for the Arts in Public 
Places Program. 


(b) The Culver City Arts Committee may 
recommend to the City Council the purchase of 
art work to be displayed on public property. A 
recommendation shall include the type of art 
work considered, an analysis of the constraints 
applicable to placement of the art work on a 
site, the need for and practicality of the mainte- 
nance of the art work, and the costs of acquisi- 
tion and installation of the art work. 


Section 33H-33. Return of Fees. 


(a) Fees paid into the City Art Fund which 
are not committed within five (5) years from the 
date of payment may be returned to the then 
current owner of the development project, with 
all interest actually earned thereon, if a written 
request for return is filed with the City Clerk 
during the fifth year after payment, and refund 
of the fees is approved by the City Council. The 
request for return shall be verified, and include 
the date of payment, the amount paid and 
method of payment, the location of the new 
development for which the fee was paid, and a 
statement that the applicant is the payor of the 
fees or the current owner of the development 
project. 


N11 


(b) The City Council shall determine if 
return of the then uncommitted portion of the 
fees and interest is appropriate and, if so, the 
method of refund. No refund shall be appropri- 
ate if the City Council determines any one of the 
following applies: 


(1) The City Council finds the fee is 
needed for the Art in Public Places Pro- 
gram. 


(2) Moneys were not posted as fees, but 
were satisfied by letter of credit, bond or 
other instrument taken to secure payment at 
a future date. 


(3) The administrative costs of refunding 
uncommitted fees pursuant to this Article 
exceeds the amount to be refunded; pro- 
vided notice of a public hearing on this 
issue has been published and posted on the 
site of the development project in not less 
than three places 


SECTION 3. This Ordinance shall take effect thirty 
(30) days from the date of its adoption and prior to the 
expiration of fifteen (15) days from the adoption hereof 
the City Clerk shall cause this Ordinance to be published 
in the Culver City News.

1988.
